Hence, instead of working with the descent equation, we can work with this inequality. However, there are two problems here: First, unlike the descent equation, it is not immediately obvious that unraveling this inequality will allow us to terminate after finitely many steps.
Second, even if we prove that it terminates, our ultimate aim is to relate
L_{A,g}
to ordinal-indexed functions in the Cichoń hierarchy. It is not clear how unraveling this inequality will allow us to accomplish that.

It turns out we can solve both these problems using a single solution: To each nested multiset nwqo, we will associate an
ordinal measure called its
order type
. We can use this order type to further over-approximate the terms in the above inequality to get a new inequality.
The order types will help us to prove that the unraveling of this new inequality will terminate and will also help us to relate
L_{A,g}
to functions in the Cichoń hierarchy.

4.5
Order Type and Derivatives

To each nested multiset nwqo
A
, we can associate an ordinal
o(A)
, called its
order type
as follows:

o(\Gamma_{0})=0\quad o(A+B)=o(A)\oplus o(B)\quad o(\mathsf{FM}({A}))=\omega^{o(A)}

For instance,
o(\Gamma_{1})=o(\mathsf{FM}({\Gamma_{0}}))=\omega^{0}=1
. Similarly,
o(\Gamma_{k})=k
and
o(\mathsf{FM}({\Gamma_{k}}))=\omega^{k}
. It is easy to see by structural induction that for two distinct (i.e. not isomorphic) nwqos
A,B
, we have
o(A)\neq o(B)
.
Hence, this mapping is injective. It turns out that it is also surjective, i.e., for any ordinal
\alpha<\epsilon_{0}
, we can assign a nested multiset nwqo as follows: Let
\alpha
in CNF be
\omega^{\beta_{1}}+\cdots+\omega^{\beta_{m}}
.
Then we set
C(\alpha)=\Gamma_{0}
if
\alpha=0
and
C(\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{FM}({C(\beta_{i})})
otherwise. By construction,
o
and
C
are bijective inverses of each other (modulo isomorphism of nwqos).

Recall that one of our two goals is to replace Equation
1
with a new inequality using order types. To that end, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a
derivative operator

\delta_{n}
on ordinals.

Definition 28

(Derivative Operator)
.

Given
n\geq 1
, we define:

•

D_{n}(\omega^{0})=0

•

D_{n}(\omega^{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\beta_{i}})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\oplus_{k\neq i}\beta_{k}\oplus\beta_{i}^{j}}
where for each
1\leq i\leq d
,
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\beta_{i}^{j}}=D_{n}(\omega^{\beta_{i}})
.

•

D_{n}(\omega^{\omega^{\beta}})=\omega^{\beta\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\beta})}\cdot n

•

\delta_{n}(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\omega^{\beta_{i}})=\{D_{n}(\omega^{\beta_{i}})\oplus\sum_{j\neq i}\omega^{\beta_{j}}:i=1,\cdots,d\}

By induction, it is easy to see that if
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)
then
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
.
We also note the symmetry between the definition of
R_{n}(A,a)
and
\delta_{n}
, in the way that there is a clear correspondence between each of the four cases in both these definitions. This is not by accident; the
\delta_{n}
operators are constructed exactly in such a way so that the following lemma is true. (See Appendix
F.3
for more details).

Lemma 29
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
a\in A_{\leq n}
for some
A\neq\Gamma_{0}
.
Then, there exists some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(o(A))
such that

R_{n}(A,a)\hookrightarrow C(\alpha^{\prime})
.

By combining Equation
1
and Lemma
29
, we get the following inequality, which solves our first goal.

L_{A,g}(n)\leq\max_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(o(A))}\{1+L_{C(\alpha^{\prime}),g}(g(n))\}

(2)

We will now move on to our second goal, namely using order types to relate
L_{A,g}
to functions in the fast-growing hierarchy.

4.6
Upper Bounds in the Fast-Growing Hierarchy

For any
\alpha<\epsilon_{0}
, define

M_{\alpha,g}(n)=\max_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)}\{1+M_{\alpha^{\prime},g}(g(n))\}

Equation
2
tells us that
L_{A,g}(n)\leq M_{o(A),g}(n)
. In other words, if
\alpha=o(A)
, we have
L_{C(\alpha),g}(n)\leq M_{\alpha,g}(n)
. Hence, bounding
M_{\alpha,g}(n)
will allow us to bound
L_{A,g}(n)
. We now bound
M_{\alpha,g}
by functions from the Cichoń hierarchy
(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\epsilon_{0}}
where
h(x)=x\cdot g(x)
.
First, we set up some notation and prove a couple of intermediate results.

We say that an ordinal
\alpha
is
\ell
-lean if either
\alpha=0
or
\alpha=\omega^{\beta_{1}}\cdot c_{1}+\cdots+\omega^{\beta_{m}}\cdot c_{m}
in strict CNF
with each
c_{i}\leq\ell
and
\beta_{i}
is
\ell
-lean for every
i
. We now have the following proposition, which can be proved by analysing the shape of the ordinals appearing in the
\delta_{n}
operator. (See Appendix
F.4
for more details).

Proposition 30
.

Suppose
\Omega_{k}\leq\alpha<\Omega_{k+1}
and
\alpha
is
\ell
-lean for some
l
.
If
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)
then

\alpha^{\prime}
is
\ell+\ell nk
-lean. Furthermore, if
\alpha=\omega^{\beta}
for some
\beta
, then

\alpha^{\prime}=D_{n}(\alpha)
is
\ell nk
-lean.

We are now in a position to bound
M_{\alpha,g}
by means of
h_{\alpha}
.
(See Appendix
F.4
for more details).

Lemma 31
.

Let
\alpha<\Omega_{k+1}
be
\ell
-lean. Then
M_{\alpha,g}(n)\leq h_{\alpha}(\ell+\ell nk)
where
h(x)=x\cdot g(x)
.

The following theorem is now immediate from our observation that
L_{A,g}\leq M_{o(A),g}
.

Theorem 32
.

Let
A
be a nested multiset nwqo of order type
\alpha<\Omega_{k+1}
such that
\alpha
is
\ell
-lean.
Then,
L_{A,g}(n)\leq h_{\alpha}(\ell+\ell nk)
.

Now, suppose we have a finite set
Q\equiv\Gamma_{\ell}
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consider the sequence of nested multiset nwqos
\mathcal{M}_{k},\mathcal{M}_{k-1},\cdots,\mathcal{M}_{0}
defined in Subsection
4.3
.
By construction it follows that
o(\mathcal{M}_{i})<\Omega_{k-i+1}
and
o(\mathcal{M}_{i})
is
\ell
-lean. Hence,
L_{\mathcal{M}_{0},g}(n)\leq h_{o(\mathcal{M}_{0})}(\ell+\ell nk)
. Since
o(\mathcal{M}_{0})<\Omega_{k+1}
and
o(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is
\ell
-lean, it follows that

h_{o(\mathcal{M}_{0})}(\ell+\ell nk)\leq h_{\Omega_{k+1}}(\ell+\ell nk)

[
43
, Lemma B.1, Lemma C.9]
. This proves Theorem
22
and concludes the proof of upper bound for coverability of
k
-NRCS.

5
Applications

We now use the techniques that we have developed in this paper to provide improved upper bounds
for a hierarchy of various problems from XML processing, graph transformation systems,
\pi
-calculus, parameterized verification and logic. Furthermore, we use our newly introduced model of NRCS to prove that the above hierarchy of problems from parameterized verification and logic are also complete problems for the hierarchy
\{\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
This demonstrates the efficacy of the model of NRCS as a master problem
from which reductions can be easily made.

5.1
Upper Bounds for Systems over Bounded-Depth Graphs

Our upper bound techniques developed in Section
4
are more broadly applicable than just for the model of
k
-NRCS, as we shall now demonstrate.
For this, we first set up some notation.

A
k
-depth graph
G
is any graph that can be obtained by taking a tree
T
of height at most
k
and adding edges between a node and its descendants. (Note that no edges are added between two nodes that lie in different subtrees of
T
). We can extend the induced subgraph ordering from trees of height
k
to
k
-depth graphs in a natural way.
It turns out that the induced subgraph relation
\leq_{is}
is also a nwqo on
k
-depth graphs for any
k

[
15
,
4
]
.

With this in mind, we can now consider classes
of transitions systems operating over labelled
k
-depth graphs. Formally, let

\mathcal{C}
be any class of transition systems such that each
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{C}
is a transition system
(C,\rightarrow)
where
C
consists of all
k
-depth labelled graphs with labels from some finite set
Q
and
\rightarrow\subseteq C\times C
is the step relation. Similar to
k
-NRCS, we can define the notions of
\xrightarrow{*}
, reachability and coverability for such systems.

Now, just like
k
-NRCS, if
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the compatibility and the effective predecessor basis properties, then by the same arguments given in Subsection
4.2
, it would follow that
\mathcal{C}
is well-structured, coverability is decidable and the running time of the coverability algorithm is bounded by the length of controlled bad sequences for labelled
k
-depth graphs under the
\leq_{is}
nwqo. Using existing results, we can show that there is a reflection
from labelled
k
-depth graphs with labels from some set
Q
to
labelled trees of height at most
k
with labels from the set
Q\times 2^{\{0,\dots,k-1\}}

[
4
, Section A]
. (See also Appendix
G
).

Because of this reflection, we can conclude that upper bounds for length functions
over
k
-depth graphs can be obtained from upper bounds for length functions over trees of height at most
k
. As shown in subsection
4.3
, the latter is bounded by
F_{\Omega_{k}}(p(n))
for some primitive recursive function
p
. This would then put the coverability problem for
\mathcal{C}
in the class
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
.

We can use this observation on various classes of systems from the literature.
For instance, motivated by XML processing of semi-structured data, the authors of
[
23
]
introduced
tree rewriting pattern systems
(TRPS), which are a class of systems over trees. They showed that a subclass of TRPS called positive,
k
-depth-bounded TRPS have as configurations, labelled trees of height at most
k
and possess the compatibility property as well as the effective predecessor basis property. Hence, by our results it follows that

Theorem 33
.

Coverability
1
1

1

In
[
23
]
the authors call this the pattern reachability problem
for positive
k
-depth-bounded TRPS is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
.

Similarly, the authors of
[
32
]
consider a class of graph transformation systems working over
k
-depth graphs, called
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-restricted systems, for which the above argument applies and so we get,

Theorem 34
.

Coverability for
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-restricted graph transformation systems is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
.

Finally, in
[
40
]
, it was shown that
k
-depth-bounded
\pi
-calculus processes can be reduced to reasoning over trees of height at most
2^{k}-1
, and so by the above argument we can conclude

Theorem 35
.

Coverability for
k
-depth-bounded
\pi
-calculus processes is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{2^{k}-1}}
.

We note that the best upper bounds that could be deduced from the literature prior to our work were
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{2k+1}}
for the first two problems and
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{2^{k+1}-1}}
for the last one
[
27
]
. Hence, our results significantly improve upon these bounds. We now show more such applications in the next subsections, along with matching lower bounds.

5.2
Bounded-Depth Broadcast Networks

The next application that we give is for the model of bounded-depth broadcast networks

2
2

2

They are sometimes called bounded-path broadcast networks in the literature, but since they also operate on bounded-depth graphs, for the sake of uniformity, we call them bounded-depth broadcast networks here.
.

A
k
-depth broadcast network
[
13
]
is a model of concurrent computation, in which we have finite-state agents situated on the nodes of a
k
-depth graph, called the communication topology.
All the agents execute the same finite-state protocol and initially begin at some initial state of the protocol. In each step, an agent can communicate with its neighbors by means of broadcasts, i.e., in each step, some agent sends a message which is immediately received by all of its neighbors. Therefore, configurations
of a
k
-depth broadcast network
\mathcal{P}
are labelled
k
-depth graphs with labels from some finite set
Q
of states for the agents. Such networks are known to satisfy the
compatibility as well as the effective predecessor basis property.

The coverability problem for such networks is slightly different from the usual notion of coverability, because we no longer have one initial configuration, but an infinite set of initial configurations, which are all possible
k
-depth graphs with labels
from a given set
Q_{init}\subseteq Q
of initial states. Nevertheless the usual coverability algorithm can be used for such networks (with the final check being over the set of all initial configurations)
[
13
, Section 5]
. Just as for the other models, the running time depends primarily on the length of controlled bad sequences for
k
-depth graphs. Hence, we get that

Theorem 36
.

Coverability for
k
-depth broadcast networks is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
.

Similarly, if we restrict the network to only operate over communication topologies of
k
-depth trees, i.e., trees of height at most
k
, we get that

Theorem 37
.

Coverability for
k
-depth broadcast networks over trees is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
.

We also show that both these problems are
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-hard, by giving a reduction from coverability of
k
-NRCS to both these problems. The hardness result is obtained by taking an existing reduction from
k
-NCS, i.e.,
k
-NRCS without resets to
k
-depth broadcast networks. We utilise this reduction along with the fact that broadcasts can be used to simulate
resets of counters
[
17
]
to give an improved reduction from
k
-NRCS to
k
-depth broadcast networks. (See Appendix
H
for more details).

Theorem 38
.

Coverability for
k
-depth broadcast networks and
k
-depth broadcast networks over trees are
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-complete.

Previously the best known result was a
\mathbf{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}
-completeness result for these problems, when
k
is part of the input
[
4
, Theorem 16]
. Our new result is a refined parameterized analogue of this, giving a completeness result for each fixed
k
.

5.3
Freeze LTL with Ordered Attributes

Finally, we also use our results to present an application related to an extension of LTL
called
freeze LTL with ordered attributes

[
12
]
.
This is an extension of the usual LTL to work over
data words
instead of words, which we now explain. (Our presentation is slightly different, but equivalent to the one given in
[
12
]
).

Fix some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a finite set of atomic propositions
\Sigma

and an infinite set
\mathbb{D}
.
A
k
-attributed data letter (or simply data letter) is a tuple
(A,d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
where
A\subseteq\Sigma
and each
d_{i}\in\mathbb{D}
. A data word is a sequence of data letters.
Intuitively, a data word is an extension of the usual notion of words to deal with
infinitely many letters. Freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes is an extension of LTL to talk about such data words. In addition to the usual boolean and LTL operators
\land,\lor,\lnot,X,U,F
and
G
, it also has freeze mechanisms
\uparrow^{1},\uparrow^{2},\dots,\uparrow^{k},\downarrow^{1},\downarrow^{2},\dots,\downarrow^{k}
.
Intuitively, the application of
\downarrow^{i}
at a letter
(A,d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
at some position along a word allows us to “remember” the data values
d_{1},\dots,d_{i}
.
If we have applied
\downarrow^{i}
to remember data values
d_{1},\dots,d_{i}
at some point, we can apply
\uparrow^{i}
at some later position
(B,d^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d^{\prime}_{k})
along the word. This would allow us to “compare”
d^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d^{\prime}_{i}
with the already remembered
(d_{1},\dots,d_{i})
and check if they are equal. The formal syntax and semantics is given in Appendix
I.1
.

Given a freeze LTL formula with
k
-ordered attributes, we say that it is satisfiable if there is some data word that satisfies it. The satisfiability problem is to decide whether a given freeze LTL formula is satisfiable. By giving reductions to and from the coverability problem for
k
-NRCS we show that

Theorem 39
.

The satisfiability problem for freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes is
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-complete.

To prove the upper bound, we use a result of
[
12
, Theorem 12]
which reduces this problem
to the coverability problem for
(k+1)
-NCS, i.e.,
(k+1)
-NRCS without resets.
Furthermore, the particular
(k+1)
-NCS that is constructed during the reduction has the property that, in any reachable configuration, there are at most 2 children of the root. We show that any such
(k+1)
-NCS can be simulated by a
k
-NRCS.
The intuition is that in the
k
-NRCS that we construct,
the root will simply absorb its 2 children without explicitly keeping them as nodes.
Operations involving these 2 children nodes in the original
(k+1)
-NCS, can then be simulated by reset operations. This allows us to save a level, thereby giving us a
k
-NRCS.

To prove the lower bound, we use a result of
[
12
, Theorem 13]
which gives a reduction
from coverability for
k
-NCS to satisfiability for freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes.
The intuition is that the sequence of configurations in a run of a
k
-NCS can be encoded
in the reverse
as a data word,
which can then be checked by a freeze LTL formula. We show that that reduction can be generalized to
k
-NRCS by a modification taking into account reset transitions.
For this, we need to ensure that in certain configurations representing the aftermath of firing a reset transition, there is no occurrence of a particular atomic proposition. This in turn can be encoded using the global operator
G
of LTL.

Prior to our work, the best known upper and lower bounds for freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes were
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{2(k+1)}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k-1}}
. Our results improve upon both these fronts and give tight parameterized bounds with respect to the parameter
k
.

6
Conclusion

We have provided the first natural collection of complete problem for the hierarchy
(\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, namely coverability for
k
-NRCS. Furthermore, to prove our upper bounds, we have also developed length function theorems for nested applications of the multiset operator on nwqos.
Finally, we also presented various applications of our upper bound techniques and the newly introduced model of NRCS to improve upper bounds for various other problems and identify
new complete problems for
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
, for all
k
.

Appendix A
Proof for Subsection
3.1

Assuming Theorems
4
and
5
, we show
how to get an
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
lower bound for
k
-NRCS. To this end, we first formally define a
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}
-bounded Minsky machine.

The simplest way to define a Minsky machine in our setting is to define it
as a tuple
M=(Q,\delta_{u},\delta_{z})
where
(Q,\delta_{u})
is a 1-NCS
and
\delta_{z}\subseteq Q^{3}
is a finite set of
zero-test
transitions,
each of which, we shall denote by
p_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}q_{0}

Configurations of
M
are defined exactly like configurations of
(Q,\delta_{u})
.
In addition to the semantics of steps from
\delta_{u}
, we also have steps induced
by the zero-test transitions from
\delta_{z}
as follows.

Let
t=p_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,z,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}q_{0}
be a zero-test. We say that
t
can be fired from a configuration
C
iff in
C
the root is labelled by
p_{0}
and it has no children labelled by
p
. After firing
t
,
C
can move to
C^{\prime}
, where the only difference is that the root is now labelled by
q_{0}
.

The intuitive idea is that Minsky machines represent machines with the usual counters (where the number of children of the root labelled by some label
p
represents the value of the counter
p
). The machine can then increment or decrement these counters by means of transitions in
\delta_{u}
and test them for zero by means of transitions in
\delta_{z}
.

The coverability problem for
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}
-bounded Minsky machines is defined as follows:
Given a Minsky machine
M=(Q,\delta_{u},\delta_{z})
and two of its states
p,q
,
decide whether the configuration with a singleton node
q_{init}
can cover the configuration with a singleton node
q_{f}
, along an
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}(|Q|)
bounded run. Here a
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}(|Q|)
bounded run is a run in which all the configurations satisfy the property that the total number of children of the root is at most
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}(|Q|)
.
Intuitively this corresponds to bounding the total value of all the counters along a run
by
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}(|Q|)
and then checking for coverability along such bounded runs.
This problem is well-known to be
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-hard
[
47
]
.

Assuming Theorems
4
and
5
, we now show
how to reduce the coverability problem for
H^{\Omega_{k+1}}
-bounded Minsky machines
to the coverability problem for
k
-NRCS. To this end, let
M
be a Minsky machine
with states
Q
and let
q_{init},q_{f}\in Q
. Without loss of generality,
by adding extra decrement and zero-test transitions if necessary,
we can assume that the machine can cover
q_{f}
from
q_{init}
only when all the counter values are 0, i.e., only when it has no children.

A.0.1
Convert
M
into a
1
-NRCS
\mathcal{N}

First, we modify the Minsky machine
M
so that 1) all the zero-test transitions are now reset transitions and 2) any increment/decrement to a label
p
is accompanied by a corresponding decrement/increment to the label
\#
. The first one is trivial to do. For the second one, we simply replace each transition
(s,p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(s^{\prime})

with
(s,p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(s^{\prime},\#)
and

with
(s,\#)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(s^{\prime},p)
. Let
\mathcal{N}
be this new

-NRCS.

We say that a step

in
\mathcal{N}

t=p_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}q_{0}
is honest if the number of children labelled by
p

in

is 0. We say a run is honest iff all the reset steps in it are honest.

For any configuration

of

and for any

, we can assign

. For any configuration

, let

. The following Fundamental Property of

is easy

If

then
|C^{\prime}|+n^{\prime}\leq|C|+n
. Furthermore,

|C|^{\prime}+n^{\prime}=|C|+n
iff the run is honest.

It is clear that any run in

between two configurations

where

corresponds to a honest run between

and
(C^{\prime},B)
in

and vice versa. We will now use this property along with
\mathcal{N}^{fwd}
and
\mathcal{N}^{bwd}
as follows.

A.0.2
Chaining
\mathcal{N}^{fwd},\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{bwd}

Set
\ell=|Q|,\alpha=(\Omega_{k+1})_{\ell}
. Note that

. Now use Theorems

and

to construct

We now construct a

,

by means of the following transition.

(\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{init})

By Theorem

, i.e., the number of children

.

After firing the above transition, it starts running the machine
\mathcal{N}
. Note

(q_{f})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\omega)

By the Fundamental Property of
\mathcal{N}
, it follows that the number of children
with label

that the machine can have at this point is at most
n_{2}\leq n_{1}
. In addition,
n_{2}=n_{1}
iff this simulation of
\mathcal{N}

\mathcal{N}
.
At this point, the configuration of this machine must look like

.
(This is because, we had assumed that any transition leading to

, which would leave us only with the subtrees corresponding to terms of the CNF of

Now the machine simulates

from

H^{\alpha_{2}}(n_{3})\leq H^{\alpha_{1}}(n_{2})\leq H^{\alpha_{1}}(n_{1})\leq H^{\alpha}(|Q|)
.

If this claim were true, then

.
This would then prove that if

can be covered in

. Since the converse is also easily seen to be true by construction (for instance, by first simulating

Hence all that is left is to prove the above claim. To prove this, we just need

iff

T_{\alpha_{2}}\geq_{is}T_{\alpha}
and

.
We now use Fact 5.2 and Proposition 5.4 of

]
, to get
3
3

3

27
]
can only be applied when

by the so-called structural ordering in
[
27
]
, but this ordering is the same as the induced subgraph ordering between

.

For any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},n,n^{\prime}
,

Applying this proposition to

and

and using the fact that

we get that

,
which proves the claim.

Appendix B
General Remark on Notation

Before we proceed to the Hardy computations and the constructions of the gadgets, we make a general remark as follows. Throughout the description of the construction of these gadgets,
we will be working with various encoders. Recall that an encoder for an ordinal
\alpha

is a tree that looks exactly like
T_{\alpha}
, except that some of the
\omega
labels
could be renamed. Recall that the labels in the last level in
T_{\alpha}
could be of the form
a\in\omega^{0},\dots,\omega^{\ell}
, which represent that in
T^{\prime}_{\alpha}
they had
0,\dots,\ell
nodes as children. We are only allowed to rename them as some label of the form
(n,a)

where
n
is some new label, i.e., new names for such nodes must retain the label in
T_{\alpha}
somewhere in their notation. This will pose some notational challenge in the gadgets and the constructions that we describe below, because every time we introduce a transition, we would then have to go into a case analysis on whether this transition hits the last level or not. This would make it extremely cumbersome to state the constructions.

For this reason, whenever we express transitions working over encoders, if the transitions reference the last level, we will just treat is a single label
n
and not as a pair
(n,a)
.
This is done purely for the sake of convenience, as the behavior of the transitions that we construct is (mostly) oblivious to the exact
i\in\{0,\dots,\ell\}
that is present in the label
\omega^{i}
in the original tree
T_{\alpha}
this last level.
Indeed, for the last level
k
, suppose any one of our transitions is presented as

(p_{0},\dots,p_{k})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{k})
.
This must be interpreted as follows

•

If
p_{k}=q_{k}=\omega

then this actually represents

\ell+1
many transitions,
(p_{0},\dots,\omega^{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,\omega^{i})

for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
.

•

If
p_{k}=\omega
and
q_{k}\neq\omega
, then this actually represents

\ell+1
many transitions

(p_{0},\dots,\omega^{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,(q_{k},{\omega^{i}})))
for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
,

•

If
p_{k}\neq\omega
and
q_{k}=\omega
, then this actually represents

\ell+1
many transitions

(p_{0},\dots,(p_{k},{\omega^{i}})))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,\omega^{i})
for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
.

•

If
p_{k}\neq\omega
and
q_{k}\neq\omega
, then this actually represents
\ell+1
many transitions

(p_{0},\dots,(p_{k},{\omega^{i}})))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,(q_{k},{\omega^{i}}))
for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
.

Similarly, if we have a transition
(p_{0},\dots,p_{k})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})

with
j<k
, then for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
, this must be interpreted as
(p_{0},\dots,\omega^{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
if
p_{k}=\omega^{i}

and as
(p_{0},\dots,(p_{k},{\omega^{i}}))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
if
p_{k}\neq\omega^{i}
. The case of
(p_{0},\dots,p_{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{k})

with
j<k
must be interpreted as
(p_{0},\dots,p_{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,\omega^{0})
.

Finally, if we say that we want to add a child to a node at the
k^{th}
level with label
\omega
, then for each
0\leq i\leq\ell
, this must be interpreted as changing the labels
of
\omega^{i}
and
(p,{\omega^{i}})
(for any
p
) to
\omega^{i+1}
and
(p,{\omega^{i+1}})

respectively if
i+1<\ell
. Similarly in the case of decrements it must be moved to
i-1
and in the case of resets, it must be moved to 0.

With this notation stated, we now move on to describing our constructions.

Appendix C
Proofs for Subsection
3.2
: Proof of Theorem
4

In this section, we shall show that using the smallest-child gadgets and the copy gadgets
we can construct
\mathcal{N}^{fwd}
. We have already defined
small-initialized and lossy smallest-child encoders and smallest-child gadgets in Subsection
3.2
. We have also defined marked and lossy copy encoders in
Subsection
3.2
. The only thing left to define
is the notion of an
k
-copy gadget, which we define as follows.

Definition 41

(Copy Gadgets)
.

A
k
-copy gadget is a
k
-NRCS such that for any
\alpha\leq(\Omega_{k+1})_{\ell}
and any marked encoder
T
for
\alpha
:

•

There exists a run from
T
to the perfect copy encoder of
T
.

•

Suppose there exists a run from
T
to some
T^{\prime}
such that the root of
T^{\prime}
is labeled by
end_{copy}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is a lossy copy encoder of
T
.

Now, assuming that we can construct
i
-smallest-child and
i
-copy gadgets for any
i\leq k
,
we shall now show how to prove the following.

See
4

The construction of
\mathcal{N}^{fwd}
is as follows: It initially guesses whether the
current tree
C_{\alpha,n}
is such that
\alpha
represents either a successor ordinal
\beta+1

or a limit ordinal
\lambda
. For the former case, we need to move to
C_{\beta,n+1}
.
To do this, we simply need to remove a child of the root marked by
\omega
and convert it into
\#
. This can be done by

(\omega,\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}({succ})

({succ})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\omega,\#)

we get

.

Suppose we guess that our current configuration is

for some limit ordinal

and if it represents an exponent with a successor ordinal, we need to reduce it by one and copy it

times;

(\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{sm})

[If the node labelled by

Appendix D
Proofs for Subsection
3.2
: Proof of Theorem
5

In this section, we shall show how to construct
\mathcal{N}^{bwd}
. To construct this, we will need one more type of gadget in addition to the smallest-child gadget. This is the
comparator gadget
which lets us compare two different ordinals. (This is the same comparator gadget that we had mentioned in Subsection
3.3
).
Similar to the other gadgets, we first define the encoders on which they will act and then define the gadget formally.

The first encoder we need is called a
comparator encoder
, which will intuitively allow us to mark two subtrees (corresponding to two different ordinal terms in the CNF, which we will later compare against each other).

Definition 42
.

A
comparator encoder
for an ordinal
\alpha
is an encoder for
\alpha
whose root is labeled by
start_{cmp}
and exactly two of its children are labeled by
A_{cmp}
and
B_{cmp}
, respectively.

The children marked by
A_{cmp}
and
B_{cmp}
correspond to the terms in the CNF of
\alpha
which we want to compare. Ultimately, we want gadgets that will start from a comparator encoder for
\alpha
and decide which one of the two ordinals corresponding to the two marked subtrees is bigger. To this end, we define a
lossy compared encoder
as follows.

Definition 43
.

Given a comparator encoder
T
for
\alpha
, a
lossy compared encoder

for
T
is a tree
T^{\prime}
that is exactly like
T
except for the following changes:

•

Let
v_{A},v_{B}
be the nodes in
T
labeled by
A_{cmp}
and
B_{cmp}
, respectively. Then
T^{\prime}
contains all the nodes of
T
, except possibly for some nodes in the subtrees rooted at
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
.

•

Every node in
T^{\prime}
has the same label as its corresponding node in
T
,
except that the root is labeled by
end_{cmp}
, and
v_{A},v_{B}
are labeled as follows.

–

If the ordinals represented by the subtrees rooted at
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
in
T^{\prime}
are the same, then they are both labeled by
equal_{cmp}
.

–

If the ordinal of one subtree is bigger than the ordinal of the other subtree, then the node with the bigger subtree is labeled by
big_{cmp}
and the other one by
small_{cmp}
.

A lossy compared encoder is called a
perfect compared encoder
iff

T^{\prime}
contains all the nodes of
T
.

We can now define the notion of a comparator gadget as follows.

Definition 44

(Comparator Gadget)
.

A
k
-comparator gadget is a
k
-NRCS such that for any
\alpha<(\Omega_{k+1})_{\ell}
and any comparator encoder
T
for
\alpha
, the following holds:

•

There exists a run from
T
to the perfect compared encoder of
T
.

•

Suppose there exists a run from
T
to some
T^{\prime}
such that the root of
T^{\prime}
is labeled by
end_{cmp}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is a lossy compared encoder of
T
.

Note that, similar to smallest-child gadgets, here we allow the comparator gadget to make lossy errors. Because of these lossy errors, we are only guaranteed that the ordinals represented by the nodes
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
in
T^{\prime}
(not in
T
!) are correctly marked as big or small.

Now, assuming that we can construct
i
-smallest-child and
i
-comparator gadgets for any
i\leq k
, we shall now show how to prove the following.

See
5

Similar to the forward case, the backward case initially guesses whether the current tree is of the form
C_{\alpha,n+1}
or of the form
C_{\lambda_{n},n}
. In the former case,
we need to move to
C_{\alpha+1,n}
and this can be easily done as follows:

(\omega,\#)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\omega,\omega)

In the latter case, we need to move to
C_{\lambda,n}
. For this we need to convert

\lambda_{n}
into
\lambda
. Note that
\lambda
could either be of the form
\gamma+\omega^{\lambda^{\prime}}
or of the form
\gamma+\omega^{\beta+1}
. Hence,
\lambda_{n}

is either of the form
\gamma+\omega^{\lambda^{\prime}_{n}}
or of the form
\gamma+\omega^{\beta}\cdot n
. In the first case, we need to identify the subtree corresponding
to the smallest term
\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
and recursively convert
\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
into
\lambda
.
In the second case, we need to identify
(n-1)
other copies of the subtree corresponding
to the smallest term
\beta
, remove all of them and convert the remaining copy into
\beta+1
. To identify
n-1
other copies of the same ordinal, we use the comparator gadget
n-1
times
and remove one copy for each invocation of the comparator gadget.
We now implement these steps as follows, in a way that is symmetric to the forward case.

First, we change the label of the root to mark that we are in the
\lambda_{n}
case:

(\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{sm})

We will now proceed recursively in at most
k
phases. For each
0\leq i<k
, we assume at the beginning of the
i^{th}
phase that we have marked a unique path
v_{0},\cdots,v_{i}

in the configuration by the labels
(\Phi_{i},start_{sm})
where
\Phi_{i}:=\underbrace{\textit{bwd}_{i},\cdots,\textit{bwd}_{i}}_{i}
. Now, we proceed to invoke the
(k-i)
-smallest-child gadget on
v_{i}
.
This is similar to the forward case, where we take that gadget and prepend
\Phi_{i}
to each transition. We then wait for the gadget computation to end, which happens when
v_{i}

gets the label
end_{sm}
. At this point, the smallest-child gadget would have marked the unique child of
v_{i}
(say
v_{i+1}
) by
smallest
, which represents the smallest ordinal term in the CNF of the ordinal of
v_{i}
. This smallest ordinal could either be of the form

\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
or of the form
\beta
with
n-1
other copies of it present.
If we guess the former case, we just need to go to the next phase on
v_{i+1}
.

(\Phi_{i},end_{sm},smallest)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\Phi_{i+1},start_{sm})

In the latter case,
v_{i+1}
must represent an ordinal
\beta
with
n-1
other copies of it present. Therefore, we start identifying
n-1
other subtrees whose represented ordinal is the same as
v_{i+1}
and for this we have to use the comparator gadget.
To this end, let
\Phi_{i}^{\prime}=\underbrace{\textit{bwd}_{i}^{\prime},\cdots,\textit{bwd}_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}
and
\xi_{i}=\underbrace{\textit{cm}_{i},\cdots,\textit{cm}_{i}}_{i}
. First we prepare the nodes
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
for
the comparator gadget.

(\Phi_{i},end_{sm},smallest)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\Phi_{i}^{\prime},start_{cmp},A_{cmp})

Then, we non-deterministically select a sibiling
v_{i+1}^{\prime}
of
v_{i+1}
to compare it with

(\Phi_{i}^{\prime},start_{cmp},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\xi_{i},start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

From here, we invoke the
(k-i)
-comparator gadget on the node
v_{i}
with the two marked
nodes
v_{i+1}
and
v_{i+1}^{\prime}
. This we can once again do by prepending
\xi_{i}
to every transition of that gadget. The computation of the gadget ends when
v_{i}
reaches the
state
end_{cmp}
. At this point, we want the labels of both
v_{i+1}
and
v_{i+1}^{\prime}
to be
equal_{cmp}
(in fact, by the properties of the comparator gadget, just checking for one of them will do). We then prepare the comparator gadget once again, convert an
\#
to
\#^{\prime}

(to indicate that we have found a copy of the ordinal we were searching for) and start
the process once again.

(\xi_{i},end_{cmp},equal_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert},\xi_{i-1},start_{cmp},A_{cmp})

(\textit{convert},\#)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime},\#^{\prime})

From
\textit{convert}^{\prime}
, just like in the forward case, we guess either to proceed further
to do another comparison or we guess that we have found all the other required
n-1
copies
of
v_{i+1}
. In the former case, we just need to mark another sibiling of
v_{i+1}
for the comparator gadget, from which we can call it again.

(\textit{convert}^{\prime},\xi_{i-1},start_{cmp},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\xi_{i},start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

In the latter case, we start transferring all the
\#^{\prime}
’s to
\#
, exactly like
in the forward case. (We also convert one
\#
to
\#
’s to ensure that we have not overshot and invoked the comparator gadget
n
times).

(\textit{convert}^{\prime},\#)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime},\#^{\prime})

(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime},\#^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime},\#)

(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\#^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\#^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\#^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\#^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{last}_{i})

Finally, we have to increase the ordinal
\beta
to
\beta+1
and so we have to
add one child labelled by
\omega
to the node marked with
A_{cmp}
and conclude the computation.

(\textit{last}_{i},\xi_{i-1},start_{cmp},A_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\underbrace{\omega,\cdots,\omega}_{i+3})

Appendix E
Proofs for Subsection
3.3

In this subsection, we will present the construction of the copy gadgets, smallest-child gadgets, comparator gadgets and the biggest-child gadgets. All of them have been defined either in the main part of the paper or in the preceding sections of the appendix, except for the biggest-child gadget, which we now define as follows. First, we
define a biggest-child encoder, similar to a smallest-child encoder.

A big-initialized encoder is like a small-initialized encoder except
that the root is labelled by
start_{big}
. Now, we define a lossy biggest-child encoder.

Definition 45
.

Given an initialized encoder
T
for
\alpha
, a lossy biggest-child encoder for
T

is a tree
T^{\prime}
that looks exactly like
T
, except for the following changes:

•

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be the children of the root labeled by
\omega
in
T
.
T^{\prime}
contains all the nodes of
T
, except possibly for some nodes in the subtree of any of the
v_{i}
.

•

Every node in
T^{\prime}
has the same label as its corresponding node in
T
,
except that the root is labeled by
end_{big}
, and each
v_{i}
(if it exists in
T^{\prime}
) is labeled as follows:

–

If the ordinal represented by the subtree rooted at
v_{i}
in
T^{\prime}
is the biggest among all the ordinals of any of the subtrees of all the other
v_{j}
in
T^{\prime}
, then
v_{i}
is labeled by
biggest
.

–

Otherwise,
v_{i}
has the same label as it had in
T
.

A lossy biggest-child encoder is called a
perfect biggest-child encoder
if

T^{\prime}
contains all the nodes of
T
.

Now, we can define a biggest-child gadget as follows.

Definition 46

(Biggest-child Gadgets)
.

A
k
-biggest-child gadget is a
k
-NRCS such that for any
\alpha<(\Omega_{k+1})_{\ell}
and any big-initialized encoder
T
for
\alpha
:

•

There exists a run from
T
to the perfect biggest-child encoder of
T
.

•

Suppose there exists a run from
T
to some
T^{\prime}
such that the root of
T^{\prime}
is labeled by
end_{sm}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is a lossy biggest-child encoder of
T
.

Having defined the biggest-child gadgets, we now show how to construct all the types of gadgets defined. We begin with the copy gadgets.

E.1
Proof of Theorem
11
: Constructing Copy Gadgets

Recall the intuition that we had presented for the copy gadgets - we simply do a DFS
over the subtree that we want to mark and whenever we hit a maximum branch we simply copy it onto another copy. We now describe this formally.

A copy gadget starts from a marked encoder and so this means that the root
u
is labelled by
start_{copy}
and one of its children
v_{1}
is labelled by
mrkd
. As a first step,
we change the label of
v_{1}
to
mrkd_{1}
as well as create a new child
v_{1}^{\prime}
labelled
by
mrkd^{\prime}_{1}
.

(start_{copy},mrkd)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{copy}^{\prime},mrkd_{1})

(start_{copy}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{cp}_{1},mrkd^{\prime}_{1})

Now, assume that we have two paths from the root, one as
u,v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
labelled by
\textit{cp}_{i},\Phi_{i}
where
\Phi_{i}:=\underbrace{mrkd_{i},\cdots,mrkd_{i}}_{i}
and the other as
u,v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{i}^{\prime}
labelled by

\textit{cp}_{i},\Phi_{i}^{\prime}
where
\Phi_{i}^{\prime}
is defined as
:=\underbrace{mrkd^{\prime}_{i},\cdots,mrkd^{\prime}_{i}}_{i}
. The first one is the current DFS branch along which we are exploring and the second one is where we have to copy the nodes from the first one. To this end, we either guess now to extend the branch
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}

or guess that there are no other children of
v_{i}
left to explore. In the former
case we just have to do

(\textit{cp}_{i},\Phi_{i},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{cp}_{i+1}^{\prime},\Phi_{i+1})

(\textit{cp}_{i+1}^{\prime},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{cp}_{i+1},\Phi_{i+1}^{\prime})

For the latter case, we simply need to reset all children of
v_{i}
labelled by
\omega
,
mark
v_{i}
as
\omega^{\prime}
(hence ensuring that we will not explore any branch with
v_{i}
ever again), mark
v_{i}^{\prime}
as
\omega
(hence ensuring that
v_{i}
has been copied safely in the other subtree) and move up the branch for
i>1
.

(\textit{cp}_{i},\Phi_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}_{i},\Phi_{i-1},\omega^{\prime})

(\textit{convert}_{i},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{cp}_{i-1},\Phi_{i-1}^{\prime},\omega)

On the other hand for
i=1
, moving up the branch simply means moving up from
v_{1}
, which means the DFS is now complete. So in this case we mark
v_{1}
as
\omega^{\prime}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}

as
cpd
.

(\textit{cp}_{1},mrkd_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}_{1},\omega^{\prime})

(\textit{convert}_{i},mrkd_{1}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{cp}_{0},cpd)

The construction is almost over, since the subtrees of
v_{1}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}

are the same except now all the nodes in the subtree of
v_{1}

are labelled by
\omega^{\prime}
and we want to convert them to
\omega
.
This we do (lossily) once again by a DFS on the subtree of
v_{1}

and convert every
\omega^{\prime}
to
\omega
, in exactly the same way as the construction from above.

Remark 47
.

As a final remark, as we had already mentioned in Section
B
, when dealing with nodes in the last level, we actually have to not check for nodes labelled by
\omega
, but rather
\omega^{0},\dots,\omega^{\ell}
and keep track of them when converting them to
mrkd_{i},mrkd_{i}^{\prime}
etc.

E.2
Constructing
k
-Smallest-Child and
k
-Biggest-Child Gadgets

As mentioned in Subsection
3.3
, it is quite easy to construct
a 1-comparator gadget. In this subsection, we will show how to construct
k
-smallest-child and
k
-biggest-child gadgets, assuming that a
k
-comparator gadget exists.

E.2.1
Constructing
k
-Smallest-Child Gadget

See
13

The idea is to iterate over pairs of all subtrees corresponding to terms in the CNF of the ordinal, compare them and continue with the one that is smaller. This is roughly the same idea that we used in the backward Hardy computation to identify
n-1
copies of the same ordinal. We now make this idea concrete.

A
k
-smallest-child gadget starts from a small-initialized encoder where the root is labelled by
start_{sm}
.
We ultimately want to identify the smallest ordinal represented by all the children
labelled by
\omega
. As a first step, we mark some child
v_{A}
to be compared

(start_{sm},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{sm}^{\prime},A_{cmp})

Then we mark another child
v_{B}
to be compared and run the
i
-comparator gadget

(start_{sm},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

At this point, the root is labelled by
start_{cmp}
and two of its children
v_{A},v_{B}
are labelled by

A_{cmp}
and
B_{cmp}
. We simply run the
k
-comparator gadget which ends
when the root becomes
end_{cmp}
. At that point, either both
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
are labelled by

equal_{cmp}
or exactly one of them is labelled by
small_{cmp}
and the other by
big_{cmp}
.
In the former case, we simply mark one of them (say
v_{B}
) as
\omega^{\prime}
and the other one (say
v_{A}
) as
A_{cmp}
, which is the current smallest candidate so that it can be compared to some other node next.

(end_{cmp},equal_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})

(end_{cmp}^{\prime},equal_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},A_{cmp})

In the latter case, we mark the one labelled by
big_{cmp}
(say
v_{B}
) as
\omega^{\prime}

and the other one (say
v_{A}
) as
A_{cmp}
, so that it can be compared to some other node next.

(end_{cmp},big_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})

(end_{cmp}^{\prime},small_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},A_{cmp})

At this point, in both the cases, we now guess whether there are any other nodes to be compared to
A_{cmp}

or not. In the former case, we now find another node to mark as
B_{cmp}
and run the comparator gadget again.

(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

Otherwise, we now reset all nodes labelled by
\omega
, and declare the node labelled by
A_{cmp}
as the smallest

(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{declare})

(\textit{declare},A_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert},smallest)

We now convert all the nodes labelled by
\omega^{\prime}
back to
\omega
lossily and once that is done, we set the root as
end_{sm}
, declaring the end of the
i
-smallest gadget computation.

(\textit{convert},\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert},\omega)

(\textit{convert})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime})

(\textit{convert}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{sm})

E.2.2
Constructing
k
-Biggest-Child Gadget

See
14

The idea is to pick one subtree, copy it once, and check if the ordinal of the original is bigger than the ordinals corresponding to the other subtrees.
We now make this idea concrete.

A
k
-biggest-child gadget starts from a big-initialized encoder where the root is labelled by
start_{big}
. We ultimately want to identify the biggest ordinal represented by all the children
labelled by
\omega
. As a first step, we mark a candidate node
v
non-deterministically
as the candidate for the biggest child and copy the subtree of this node.

(start_{big},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{copy},mrkd)

Now, we run a modified version of the
k
-copy gadget, which will work exactly as the original
k
-copy gadget, except instead of marking
v
as
\omega
in the end, it will mark it
as
\omega^{\prime}
. (It is straightforward to modify the construction given in Subsection
E.1
so that this holds).
We then wait till the root has received the label
end_{copy}
.
At this point,
v
will be marked by
\omega^{\prime}
and there will be another new node
v^{\prime}
labelled by
cpd
.

We now compare the subtree rooted at
v
(the only node marked by
\omega^{\prime}
)
with all the subtrees of all the nodes marked by
\omega
and check that
v
remains bigger each time. To this end, we first mark
v
as
A_{cmp}
and pick some other node labelled by
\omega
to be marked as
B_{cmp}
.

(end_{copy},\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{copy}^{\prime},A_{cmp})

(end_{copy}^{\prime},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

We now run a modified version of the
k
-comparator gadget, which will work exactly as the
original
k
-comparator gadget, except that it will keep track of the label
A_{cmp}
in the node
v
throughout. More precisely, we modify every transition
(p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{j})
of the
i
-comparator gadget in the following way:
If
p_{1}=A_{cmp}
, then we modify it so that it now becomes

(p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},(A_{cmp},q_{1}),\dots,q_{j})

If
p_{1}\neq A_{cmp}
, then we keep the transition as well as add the following transition:

(p_{0},(A_{cmp},p_{1}),\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},(A_{cmp},q_{1}),\dots,q_{j})

We do a similar change for reset transitions.
This ensures that the label
A_{cmp}
is preserved in
v
throughout in the
k
-comparator gadget. We then wait for the gadget to end, which will happen when the root is labelled
by
end_{cmp}
. At this point,
v
will be labelled
(A_{cmp},x)
and the other compared node
will be labelled by
y\in\{big_{cmp},equal_{cmp},small_{cmp}\}
. If
x\notin\{big_{cmp},equal_{cmp}\}
, then this means that the node
v
is not the biggest-child gadget and so our original guess was incorrect.
We then simply deadlock and do not move at all. If
x
does equal any one of these two choices, we relabel it back to
A_{cmp}
and relabel
y
to
\omega^{\prime}
.

(end_{cmp},(A_{cmp},x))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime},A_{cmp})

(end_{cmp}^{\prime},y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime})

We can now choose to compare
A_{cmp}
with another node labelled by
\omega
or decide
that we have compared it with everything.
In the former case, we do

(end_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

In the latter case, we reset all nodes labelled by
\omega
, convert all
\omega^{\prime}
back to
\omega
.

(end_{cmp}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert})

(\textit{convert},\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert},\omega)
