(\textit{convert})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,\omega^{\prime}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime})

We then declare the node labelled by
cpd
as the winner and remove the node labelled by
A_{cmp}
(since it is a copy of the winner).

(\textit{convert}^{\prime},cpd)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime},biggest)

(\textit{convert}^{\prime\prime},A_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(end_{big})

E.3
Constructing
(k+1)
-Comparator Gadget

See
15

The main idea is a culmination of various different ideas from different gadgets. First we isolate the biggest-grandchildren
w_{A},w_{B}
in each of the two marked children
v_{A},v_{B}
of the root. Then, we copy the subtrees of
w_{A},w_{B}
as
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
. Then, we apply the
k
-comparator gadget between
w_{A}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}^{\prime}
, whilst also ensuring
that whatever operations are being done on the subtree of
w_{A}^{\prime}
are also being done on the subtree of
w_{A}
(similarly for
w_{B}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}
). We do this to ensure that
the subtree of
w_{A}
is always an induced subtree of
w_{A}^{\prime}
at each point (similarly for
w_{B}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}
). Then, once we get the answer for
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
, we are no longer guaranteed
that they still represent the biggest-children in
v_{A},v_{B}
. So, we run another biggest-child check using
w_{A}
and
w_{B}
, which if passes, will imply that
w_{A}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}^{\prime}

are still the biggest children of
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
respectively.

Once this check passes, if
w_{A}^{\prime}
is bigger than
w_{B}^{\prime}
, then we know that
v_{A}
is bigger than
v_{B}
and vice versa. If
w_{A}^{\prime}
turns out to be equal to
w_{B}^{\prime}
then
we mark them as
\omega^{\prime}
and move on to the next biggest-child of
v_{A}
and
v_{B}

and continue the gadget. This way we will either conclude that one of
v_{A},v_{B}
is smaller than the other or we will exhaust all the children of
v_{A},v_{B}
, at which point,
we convert all of its children labelled by
\omega^{\prime}
into
\omega
and declare them as equal.
We now make this idea more concrete.

A
(k+1)
-comparator gadget begins with the root
u
labelled by
start_{cmp}

and two of its children
v_{A},v_{B}
labelled by
A_{cmp}
and
B_{cmp}
.
We first employ the
k
-biggest-child gadget on
v_{A},v_{B}
to mark the node which represents
the biggest term in the CNF of the ordinals represented by
v_{A},v_{B}
. More precisely,
we first do

(start_{cmp},A_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{A},start_{big})

and then run the
k
-biggest-child gadget on
v_{A}
. Technically, this would require us to prepend
start_{cmp}^{A}
with every transition of this gadget. At the end of this gadget,
the root
u
will be labelled by
start_{cmp}^{A}
, the node
v_{A}
will be labelled by
end_{big}

and one of its children (say
w_{A}
) will be labelled by
biggest
. We now change
end_{big}
to
start_{copy}
,

biggest
to
mrkd
and copy this subtree.

(start_{cmp}^{A},end_{big},biggest)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{A},start_{copy},mrkd)

We now run the
k
-copy gadget to copy the subtree rooted at
w_{A}
, i.e., the node with the label
mrkd
. Similar to the previous subsection, we modify the copy gadget so that it
leaves
w_{A}
as
\omega^{\prime}
and creates a copy
w_{A}^{\prime}
labelled by
cpd
. So at this point,
the root will be
start_{cmp}^{A}
,
v_{A}
will be
end_{copy}
,
w_{A}
will be
\omega^{\prime}
and
w_{A}^{\prime}

will be
cpd
. We now do the exact same steps for the node
v_{B}
, i.e., the node labelled by
B_{cmp}
. This will lead us to a tree where
u
will be
start_{cmp}^{B}
,
v_{A},v_{B}
will be
end_{copy}
,
w_{A},w_{B}
will be
\omega^{\prime}
and
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
will be
cpd
.

We now run the
k
-comparator gadget on
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
to check if one is bigger than the other.
For this, we first set them up.

(start_{cmp}^{B},end_{copy},cpd)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime},start_{cmp},A_{cmp})

(start_{cmp}^{\prime},end_{copy},cpd)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},start_{cmp},B_{cmp})

Then, we run the
k
-comparator gadgets on them. However, note that they are not actually in the same subtree. Nevertheless, we can still modify the
k
-comparator gadget in a straightforward manner so that it compares them across two different subtrees.
For instance, this would involve taking every update transition
(p_{0},\dots,p_{\ell})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
of the
i
-comparator gadget and transforming it as

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},p_{0},\dots,p_{\ell})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime},q_{0}^{\prime},\dots,q_{j})

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime},p_{0})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},q_{0})

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},q_{0}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},q_{0})

This just ensures that the labels of
v_{A},v_{B}
are the same at each step, thereby acting as if they are one node and therefore simulating as if
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
belong to the same subtree.

Furthermore, as we run the
k
-comparator gadgets on both of them, we also ensure
that any change made to
w_{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
w_{B}^{\prime}
) is also mirrored in
w_{A}
(resp.
w_{B}
).
More precisely, if we execute a transition

(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{\ell})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{j})

of the
k
-comparator gadget we replace it with

(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{\ell})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2},\dots,q_{j})

(q_{0}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime},\dots,p_{\ell})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},q_{1},\omega^{\prime},\dots,q_{j})

This ensures that whatever changes were made to the subtree rooted at
w_{A}^{\prime}
are also maintained on the subtree rooted at
w_{A}
, since
w_{A}
is the only node labelled by
\omega^{\prime}

in the subtree of
v_{A}
. (Similarly for
w_{B}
and
v_{B}
).

We then wait till the gadget finishes, which will happen when both
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
get the label
end_{cmp}
(and the root by the label
start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime}
). At this point, the nodes
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
would have been compared. Before, we check the result of this comparison, we
first check if
w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime}
are still the biggest-children in the subtrees of
v_{A},v_{B}
,
by running the
k
-biggest-child gadgets on
w_{A}
and
w_{B}
respectively. If the check passes
on
w_{A}
and
w_{B}
, then since we have guaranteed that
w_{A}
and
w_{B}
are respectively smaller than
w_{A}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}^{\prime}
, we would be guaranteed that
w_{A}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}^{\prime}
still
represent the biggest-children of
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
respectively.

To do this, we mark
w_{A}
as a candidate for the biggest child and compare it with
all the children of
v_{A}
labelled by
\omega
, in exactly the same way as we constructed
the
k
-biggest child gadget. Similarly we do this for
w_{B}
.

If these checks pass, we can destroy
w_{A},w_{B}
and then look at the results of the comparator gadget on
w_{A}^{\prime}
and
w_{B}^{\prime}
. If say
w_{A}^{\prime}
is smaller than
w_{B}^{\prime}
, then we can immediately conclude that
v_{A}
is smaller than
v_{B}
and vice versa.

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},end_{cmp},small_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime},small_{cmp},\omega)

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime\prime},end_{cmp},big_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{declare},big_{cmp},\omega)

After declaring this, we just need to convert all the
\omega^{\prime}
’s in the subtrees of
v_{A}

and
v_{B}
back to
\omega
, which we have already seen how to do before, after which we can end the computation.

On the other hand, if
w_{A},w_{B}
turn out to be equal, then we mark them as
\omega^{\prime}

and repeat the computation all over again.

(start_{cmp}^{\prime\prime},end_{cmp},equal_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(\textit{again},A_{cmp},\omega^{\prime})

(\textit{again},end_{cmp},equal_{cmp})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(start_{cmp},B_{cmp},\omega^{\prime})

This completes the construction of the
(k+1)
-comparator gadget.

Appendix F
Proofs of Section
4

F.1
Proofs of Subsection
4.2

See
20

Proof.

We discuss the case when
t
is a decrementing transition; the argument for the other transitions are similar.

Let
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
be a decrementing transition with
j<i
. Since
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
, it follows that

C
must have a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
from the root labelled by
p_{0},\dots,p_{i}
.
Furthermore, since
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
, it follows that
C^{\prime}
is the same as
C

except that the nodes
v_{0},\dots,v_{j}
are now labelled by
q_{0},\dots,q_{j}
and the subtree
rooted at
v_{i+1}
has been removed.

Now, since
C\leq_{is}D
, it follows that
D
also has the path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
from the root labelled by
p_{0},\dots,p_{i}
. Hence, we can fire the transition
t
from
D

to reach some configuration
D^{\prime}
. Since
C
is an induced subgraph of
D
,
it is then easy to see that
C^{\prime}
is an induced subgraph of
D^{\prime}
as well.
∎

See
21

Proof.

Let
C
be a configuration and
t
be a transition. Define
pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
to be the set of all configurations
C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}D
for some some
D\in\uparrow C
.
Clearly
pre(\uparrow C)=\cup_{t\in\delta_{u}\cup\delta_{r}}\ pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
.
Hence, to prove the proposition, it suffices to show the following: For every
t\in\delta_{u}\cup\delta_{t}
, in exponential time we can compute a set of configurations

C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
such that
\cup_{i=1}^{m}\uparrow C_{i}=pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
and
|C_{i}|\leq|C|+k+1

for every
i
. We will call such a collection
\{C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\}
a basis for
pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
.

We discuss the case when
t
is a decrementing transition; the argument for the other cases are similar. We claim the following, which we call Claim A:

Suppose
C^{\prime}\in pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
. Then there exists
C^{\prime\prime}

such that
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
,
|C^{\prime\prime}|\leq|C|+k+1
and
C^{\prime\prime}\in pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
.

Assuming Claim A is true, it follows that we can compute a basis
for
pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
as follows: Collect all configurations
C^{\prime\prime}
satisfying
|C^{\prime\prime}|\leq|C|+k+1
and
C^{\prime\prime}\in pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
. Let this collection be
\{C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\}
.
Note that this computation can take at most exponential time, since there can only be
exponentially many configurations
C^{\prime\prime}
satisfying
|C^{\prime\prime}|\leq|C|+k+1
.
By Claim A,
pre_{t}(\uparrow C)\subseteq\cup_{i=1}^{m}\uparrow C_{i}
.
By the compatibility property,
\cup_{i=1}^{m}\uparrow C_{i}\subseteq pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
.
Hence, we get that
\cup_{i=1}^{m}\uparrow C_{i}=pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
.

Therefore, it suffices only to prove Claim A. Suppose
C^{\prime}\in pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
for some
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
with
j<i
. Hence
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}D
for some
D\in\uparrow C
.
Since
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}D
, by definition, this means that
C^{\prime}
contains a path

v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
from the root labelled by
p_{0},\dots,p_{i}

and
D
is the same as
C^{\prime}
except that
v_{0},\dots,v_{j}
are labelled
by
q_{0},\dots,q_{j}
and the subtree at
v_{j+1}
is now missing.

Now since
D\in\uparrow C
, it follows that there must be a subset
S
of nodes of
D
such that when
D
is restricted to
S
, we get
C
.
We split this subset
S
as
S^{-}\cup S^{+}
where
S^{-}=S\cap\{v_{0},\dots,v_{j}\}

and
S^{+}=S\setminus S^{-}
. By definition of the transition
t
, note that
S^{+}
must also be present in
C^{\prime}
.

Now, we remove all nodes from
C^{\prime}
except for
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
and
S^{+}
to get a new tree
C^{\prime\prime}
. Clearly,
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
|C^{\prime\prime}|=|S^{+}|+(i+1)\leq|C|+k+1
.
Also, since we keep
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
in
C^{\prime\prime}
, we must be able to fire
t
from
C^{\prime\prime}

to reach some
D^{\prime\prime}
. It is clear that
D^{\prime\prime}
contains the nodes

v_{0},\dots,v_{j}
and
S^{+}
and so contains all of
S
. Hence,
D^{\prime\prime}\in\uparrow C

and so
C^{\prime\prime}\in pre_{t}(\uparrow C)
. This then proves Claim A.
∎

F.2
Proofs of Subsection
4.4

See
27

Proof.

The proof considers all of the four cases in the definition of
R_{n}(A,a)
. The proofs for the first and the last cases are easy to see. The other two cases
follow from an identity of
[
42
, Proposition 1]
. Because the identity in that paper is stated in different terms compared to ours, we give the complete proof here, by structural induction on
A
.

Before we begin the induction, we make a remark on notation. For any two elements
m,m^{\prime}
,
whenever it is clear from the context which nested multiset nwqo
A
they belong to, we will simply use
m\leq m^{\prime}
to denote
m\leq_{A}m^{\prime}
. This is done for the sake of brevity, as we will be dealing
with multiple complicated nested multiset nwqo expressions such as
\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{i=1}^{d}B_{i}})
.
We will similarly use
|m|
to denote
|m|_{A}
.

Case 1:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\Gamma_{0}})

In this case,
a
must be the only element of
A
. It follows then that
A/a\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{0}=R_{n}(A,a)
.

Case 2:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{i=1}^{d}B_{i}})

Let
b_{i}
denote the sub-multiset
of
a
restricted to only elements from
B_{i}
. Note that each
b\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})
and

a
is the multiset sum of
b_{1},\cdots,b_{d}
.
Furthermore, by construction the norm of each
b_{i}
is at most
n
(since the norm of
a
is at most
n
).
Let
R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),b_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}^{j}})
.
By induction hypothesis,
r_{i}:\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})/b_{i}\hookrightarrow R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),b_{i})
is a reflection for each
i
.
Furthermore, by definition,
R_{n}(A,a)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{k\neq i}B_{i}+B_{i}^{j}})
. Our task is now to come up with a reflection
r:A/a\hookrightarrow R_{n}(A,a)
. We divide this into three steps - in the first step, we define the mapping
r
;
in the second step, we show that
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
implies
m\leq m^{\prime}
;
and in the third step, we show that
|r(m)|\leq|m|
.

First step:
For each
m\in A/a
, we define an element
r(m)\in R_{n}(A,a)
as follows:
For any
1\leq k\leq d
, let
m_{k}
be the sub-multiset of
m
restricted only to elements
from
B_{k}
. Note that each
m_{k}\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{k}})
.
Furthermore, since
m\in A/a
, there must be an index
i
such that

m_{i}\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})/b_{i}
. Based on this we define
r(m)
as the sum of the multisets

\{m_{k}:k\neq i\}
and
r_{i}(m_{i})
, i.e.,

r(m)=\sum_{k\neq i}m_{k}+r_{i}(m_{i})
. By assumption
r_{i}(m_{i})\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}^{j}})
for some
1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}

and so, by construction,
r(m)
belongs to

\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{k\neq i}B_{k}+B_{i}^{j}})

and is hence an element of
R_{n}(A,a)
.
We now have to show that this satisfies the properties of a reflection.

Second step:

Suppose
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
. By assumption, this means that there is some
1\leq i\leq d
and
1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}

such that both
r(m),r(m^{\prime})\in\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{k\neq i}B_{k}+B_{i}^{j}})
. (This is because only multisets belonging to the same summand of
R_{n}(A,a)
can be compared). We want to now show that
m\leq m^{\prime}
.
For each
1\leq k\leq d
, let
m_{k}
be the sub-multiset of
m
restricted to only elements
of
B_{k}
. Similarly, we can obtain
m^{\prime}_{k}
from
m
.
By definition of
r
and by the fact that
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
, it follows that

m_{k}\leq m^{\prime}_{k}
for each
k\neq i
and
r_{i}(m_{i})\leq r_{i}(m^{\prime}_{i})
. (Once again this is because elements from
m_{k}
can only be compared with elements from
m^{\prime}_{k}
).
Since
r_{i}
is a reflection, it follows that
m_{i}\leq m^{\prime}_{i}
as well.
Hence, it follows that
m_{k}\leq m_{k}^{\prime}
for all
k
. From this it immediately follows that

m\leq m^{\prime}
.

Third step:
Let us now bound the norm of
r(m)
.
Let
r(m)=\sum_{k\neq i}m_{k}+r_{i}(m_{i})
where each
m_{k}
is the sub-multiset
of
m
restricted only to elements from
B_{k}
. Hence, by definition
of the norm function, we get that
|r(m)|=\sum_{k\neq i}|m_{k}|+|r_{i}(m_{i})|
,
By properties of a reflection, we have that
|r_{i}(m_{i})|\leq|m_{i}|
. It follows that
|r(m)|\leq\sum_{1\leq k\leq d}|m_{k}|=|m|
and so we are done.

Case 3:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\mathsf{FM}({B})})

Let
a=\Lbag a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\Rbag
. Since
|a|\leq n
, it follows that
|a_{i}|\leq n
for each
i
and
d\leq n
.
By induction hypothesis,
r_{i}:\mathsf{FM}({B})/a_{i}\hookrightarrow R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B}),a_{i})
are reflections for each
i
.
Moreover, by definition,
R_{n}(A,a)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{FM}({B\cdot(n-1)+R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B}),a_{i})})
.
Our task is now to come up with a reflection
r:A/a\hookrightarrow R_{n}(A,a)
.
Similar to the previous case, we divide this into three steps - in the first step, we define the mapping
r
;
in the second step, we show that
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
implies
m\leq m^{\prime}
;
and in the third step, we show that
|r(m)|\leq|m|
.

First step:
Let
m_{1}\in A/a
. Hence, either there must be no element
x_{1}\in m_{1}
such that
a_{1}\leq x_{1}

or there is such an element
x_{1}
and
\Lbag a_{2},\dots,a_{d}\Rbag\nleq m_{2}=m_{1}-\Lbag x_{1}\Rbag
, where
m_{1}-\Lbag x_{1}\Rbag
is the multiset obtained from
m_{1}
by decrementing
m_{1}(x_{1})
by 1.
In the latter case, either there is no element
x_{2}\in m_{2}
such that
a_{2}\leq x_{2}
or there is such an element

x_{2}
and
\Lbag a_{3},\dots,a_{d}\Rbag\nleq m_{3}=m_{2}-\Lbag x_{2}\Rbag
.
We can continue this process until
we reach an
i
such that there is no element
x_{i}\in m_{i}
such that
a_{i}\leq x_{i}
. (Such an
i
has to exist,
as otherwise we would have found
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in m_{1}
such that
a_{1}\leq x_{1},a_{2}\leq x_{2},\dots,a_{d}\leq x_{d}
,
contradicting the fact that
m_{1}\in A/a
). Hence,
m_{1}=\Lbag x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}\Rbag+m_{i}
.

We now make two observations: First, since there is no
x_{i}\in m_{i}
such that
a_{i}\leq x_{i}
, we have that

m_{i}\in A/\Lbag a_{i}\Rbag=\mathsf{FM}({\mathsf{FM}({B})/a_{i}})
. Hence, it is possible to apply the reflection
r_{i}
to each element of
m_{i}
. Therefore, we can define a mapping
s(m_{1})=\Lbag r_{i}(x):x\in m_{i}\Rbag
which gives us that
s(m_{1})\in\mathsf{FM}({R_{n}(B,a_{i})})
.
Second, suppose for each
1\leq j\leq i-1
, we have

x_{j}=\Lbag x_{j}^{1},\dots,x_{j}^{\ell_{j}}\Rbag
. Then, if we construct the multiset

s^{\prime}(m_{1})=\Lbag x:x=(j,x_{j}^{k})\text{ for some }1\leq j\leq i-1,1\leq k\leq\ell_{j}\Rbag
,
we have that
s^{\prime}(m_{1})\in\mathsf{FM}({B\cdot(n-1)})
.

Putting these two observations together,
we then define
r(m_{1})=\Lbag x:x\in s(m_{1})\text{ or }x\in s^{\prime}(m_{1})\Rbag
. Note that

r(m_{1})\in\mathsf{FM}({B\cdot(n-1)+R_{n}(B,a_{i})})

and is hence an element of
R_{n}(A,a)
. We now claim that
r
satisfies the properties of a reflection.

Second step:
Suppose
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
. By assumption, there is some
i
such that both
r(m),r(m^{\prime})\in\mathsf{FM}({(n-1)B+R_{n}(B,a_{i}))})
for some
i
. (This is because only multisets belonging to the same summand of
R_{n}(A,a)
can be compared). Hence, there must be elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}\in m

and
x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{i-1}^{\prime}\in m^{\prime}
such that
m=\Lbag x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}\Rbag+m_{i},m^{\prime}=\Lbag y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1}\Rbag+m_{i}^{\prime}
, each
a_{j}\leq x_{j},a_{j}\leq y_{j}
and
m_{i},m_{i}^{\prime}\in A/\Lbag a_{i}\Rbag
.

Let each
x_{j}=\Lbag x_{j}^{1},\cdots,x_{j}^{d_{j}}\Rbag
and let each
y_{j}=\Lbag y_{j}^{1},\cdots,y_{j}^{\ell_{j}}\Rbag
.
By construction of
r
we have that

r(m)=\Lbag x:x=r_{i}(x^{\prime})\text{ for some }x^{\prime}\in m_{i}\text{ or }x=(j,x_{j}^{k})\text{ for some }j,k\Rbag

and

r(m^{\prime})=\Lbag y:y=r_{i}(y^{\prime})\text{ for some }y^{\prime}\in m_{i}^{\prime}\text{ or }y=(j,y_{j}^{q})\text{ for some }j,q\Rbag

Since
r(m)\leq r(m^{\prime})
, it follows that there is an injection
h
from
r(m)
to
r(m^{\prime})
such that
x\leq h(x)
for all
x\in r(m)
.
By construction of
r,r^{\prime}
it follows that if
x=(j,x_{j}^{k})
for some
j,k
then
h(x)
must be
(j,y_{j}^{q})
for some
q
.
Similarly, if
x=r_{i}(x^{\prime})
for some
x^{\prime}\in m_{i}
then
h(x)
must be
r_{i}(y^{\prime})
for some
y^{\prime}\in m_{i}^{\prime}
.

We now use
h
to define an injection

w
from
m
to
m^{\prime}
such that
x\leq w(x)
for all
x\in m
.
We split the definition of
w
into two cases:

•

Suppose
x=x_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq i-1
. We then set
w(x)=y_{j}
.
We now have to prove that
x_{j}\leq y_{j}
, i.e., provide an injection
h^{\prime}

from
\Lbag x_{j}^{1},\dots,x_{j}^{d_{j}}\Rbag
to
\Lbag y_{j}^{1},\dots,y_{j}^{\ell_{j}}\Rbag

such that
x_{j}^{k}\leq h^{\prime}(x_{j}^{k})
for all
k
.
We simply take this injection to be
h
restricted to
\Lbag x_{j}^{1},\dots,x_{j}^{d_{j}}\Rbag
, i.e., if
h(j,x_{j}^{k})=(j,y_{j}^{q})
,
then
h^{\prime}(x_{j}^{k})=y_{j}^{q}
. Because
(j,x_{j}^{k})\leq(j,y_{j}^{q})
,
we have that
x_{j}^{k}\leq y_{j}^{q}
. This provides the desired injection
h^{\prime}

and so we have that
x_{j}\leq y_{j}
.

•

Suppose
x\in m_{i}
. Then, we have that
r_{i}(x)\in r(m)

and so we have that
h(r_{i}(x))=r_{i}(y)
for some
y\in m_{i}
.
We then set
w(x)=y
. By assumption on
h
, we have that
r_{i}(x)\leq r_{i}(y)
.
By properties of a reflection, we have that
x\leq y
.

Hence, we have shown that
m\leq m^{\prime}
, thereby completing this step.

Third step:
Let us now bound the norm of
r(m)
. By the same reasoning as in the previous case, it follows that

|r(m)|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq i-1}|x_{j}|+|r_{i}(m_{i})|
.
By properties of a reflection we have that
|r_{i}(m_{i})|\leq|m_{i}|
. It follows then that
|r(m)|\leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq i-1}|x_{j}|+|m_{i}|=|m|
and so we are done.

Case 4:
A=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})

Let
a\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})
for some
i
.
Note that
A/a
is then simply
\sum_{k\neq i}\mathsf{FM}({B_{k}})+\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})/a
.
By induction hypothesis, we know that
r_{i}:\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})/a\hookrightarrow R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),a)
is a reflection.
Furthermore,
R_{n}(A,a)=\sum_{k\neq i}\mathsf{FM}({B_{k}})+R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),a)
.
It is then easy to see that the mapping
r:A/a\to R_{n}(A,a)
defined by
r(a^{\prime})=a^{\prime}
if
a^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})

and
r(a^{\prime})=r_{i}(a^{\prime})
if
a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})/a
is also a reflection.
∎

F.3
Proofs of Subsection
4.5

See
29

Proof.

Let us prove by structural induction on
A
that
R_{n}(A,a)\hookrightarrow C(\alpha^{\prime})
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(o(A))
.

Case 1:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\Gamma_{0}})

In this case,
\delta_{n}(o(A))=\{D_{n}(o(A))\}=\{D_{n}(\omega^{0})\}=\{0\}
.
Since
R_{n}(A,a)=\Gamma_{0}=C(0)
, this proves the claim for this case.

Case 2:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{i=1}^{d}B_{i}})

In this case,
\delta_{n}(o(A))=\{D_{n}(o(A))\}=\{D_{n}(\omega^{\oplus_{i=1}^{d}o(B_{i})})\}
.
By definition,

D_{n}(\omega^{\oplus_{i=1}^{d}o(B_{i})})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\oplus_{k\neq i}\ o(B_{k})\oplus\beta_{i}^{j}}

where for each
1\leq i\leq d
,
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\beta_{i}^{j}}=D_{n}(\omega^{o(B_{i})})
.

Let
b_{i}
denote the sub-multiset of
a
restricted to only elements from
B_{i}
.
By induction hypothesis,
R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),b_{i})\hookrightarrow C(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\beta_{i}^{j}})=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}C(\omega^{\beta_{i}^{j}})
.
Now, by definition of
R_{n}(A,a)
and using the above property of
R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),b_{i})
, it follows that

R_{n}(A,a)\hookrightarrow\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\mathsf{FM}({\sum_{k\neq i}B_{k}+C(\beta_{i}^{j})})=C\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\omega^{\oplus_{k\neq i}\ o(B_{k})\oplus\beta_{i}^{j}}\right)

This proves the claim for this case.

Case 3:
A=\mathsf{FM}({\mathsf{FM}({B})})

In this case,
\delta_{n}(o(A))=\{D_{n}(o(A))\}=\{D_{n}(\omega^{\omega^{o(B)}})\}
.
By definition,

D_{n}(\omega^{\omega^{o(B)}})=\omega^{o(B)\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{o(B)})}\cdot n

Let
a=\Lbag a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\Rbag
. Note that
d\leq n
.
By induction hypothesis,
R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B}),a_{i})\hookrightarrow C(D_{n}(\omega^{o(B)}))
for any
a_{i}
. Now, by definition of
R_{n}(A,a)
and using the above property, it follows that

R_{n}(A,a)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{FM}({B\cdot(n-1)+C(D_{n}(\omega^{o(B)}))})\cdot n=C\left(\omega^{o(B)\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{o(B)})}\cdot n\right)

This proves the claim for this case.

Case 4:
A=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})

Let
a\in\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})
for some
i
.
In this case, by definition of
\delta_{n}(o(A)))
we have that

\alpha^{\prime}=D_{n}(o(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}})))\oplus\bigoplus_{k\neq j}o(\mathsf{FM}({B_{k}}))\in\delta_{n}(o(A))

By induction hypothesis,
R_{n}(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}),a)\hookrightarrow C(D_{n}(o(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}))))
. Now, by definition of
R_{n}(A,a)
and using the above property, it follows that

R_{n}(A,a)\hookrightarrow\sum_{k\neq i}\mathsf{FM}({B_{k}})+C(D_{n}(o(\mathsf{FM}({B_{i}}))))=C(\alpha^{\prime})

This proves the claim for this case.
∎

F.4
Proofs of Subsection
4.6

See
30

Proof.

We prove this proposition by induction on
\alpha
.

Case 1:
\alpha=1=\omega^{0}

In this case, the claim immediately follows.

Case 2:
\alpha=\omega^{\beta}
where
\beta=\omega^{\gamma}

In this case,
\alpha=\omega^{\omega^{\gamma}}
. By definition,
D_{n}(\alpha)=\omega^{\gamma\cdot(n-1)+D_{n}(\beta)}\cdot n
. Note that
\Omega_{k-1}\leq\beta<\Omega_{k}
and
\beta
is
\ell
-lean. Hence, by induction hypothesis,

D_{n}(\beta)
is
\ell n(k-1)
lean. Furthermore, since
\alpha
is
\ell
-lean, it follows that
\gamma
is also
\ell
-lean
and hence
\gamma\cdot(n-1)
is
\ell(n-1)
-lean.
It then follows that
D_{n}(\alpha)
is
\max(n,\ell(n-1)+\ell n(k-1))
-lean.
Since
\max(n,\ell(n-1)+\ell n(k-1))\leq\ell nk
, it follows that
D_{n}(\alpha)
is
\ell nk
-lean.

Case 3:
\alpha=\omega^{\beta}
where
\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\omega^{\eta_{i}}

By definition,

D_{n}(\omega^{\omega^{\eta_{i}}})=\omega^{\eta_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\eta_{i}})}\cdot n=\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\ \omega^{\eta_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\eta_{i}})}

Now, by definition,

D_{n}(\alpha)=D_{n}(\omega^{\beta})=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\omega^{\oplus_{p\neq i}\omega^{\eta_{p}}\oplus\eta_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\eta_{i}})}

(3)

Now let
\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\omega^{\gamma_{i}}\cdot c_{i}
in strict CNF.
Let us now group equal factors of
\eta_{k}
in the exponent in equation
3

and rewrite the equation as

D_{n}(\alpha)=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}(\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\omega^{\oplus_{p\neq i}\omega^{\gamma_{p}}\cdot c_{p}\oplus\omega^{\gamma_{i}}\cdot(c_{i}-1)\oplus\gamma_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})})\cdot c_{i}

(4)

Furthermore, since the
j
plays no role in the summand, it follows that

D_{n}(\alpha)=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}(\omega^{\oplus_{p\neq i}\omega^{\gamma_{p}}\cdot c_{p}\oplus\omega^{\gamma_{i}}\cdot(c_{i}-1)\oplus\gamma_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})})\cdot c_{i}n

Let

\beta_{i}=\oplus_{p\neq i}\omega^{\gamma_{p}}\cdot c_{p}\oplus\omega^{\gamma_{i}}\cdot(c_{i}-1)\oplus\gamma_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})

(5)

Note that
\beta_{i}\neq\beta_{j}
for two distinct
i,j
.
Indeed, suppose
\beta_{i}=\beta_{j}
with
i\neq j
. Then removing common terms from both sides, we are left
with the following equality

\omega^{\gamma_{j}}\oplus\gamma_{i}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})=\omega^{\gamma_{i}}\oplus\gamma_{j}\cdot(n-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{j}})

Without loss of generality, let
\gamma_{j}>\gamma_{i}
. Then, each term of the RHS is strictly less than
\omega^{\gamma_{j}}
,
but the LHS contains
\omega^{\gamma_{j}}
, which leads to a contradiction. Hence,
\beta_{i}\neq\beta_{j}
for
i\neq j
.

Therefore, since
c_{i}n\leq\ell n
, in order to prove that
D_{n}(\omega^{\beta})
is
\ell nk
-lean, it suffices to show that each
\beta_{i}
is
\ell nk
-lean. Now, each
\gamma_{p}

for
p\neq i
as well as
\gamma_{i}
and
D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})
are
\ell n(k-1)
-lean (The first two are simply
\ell
-lean by assumption and the third one follows from induction hypothesis).
Let us now analyze the coefficients that can appear in
\beta_{i}
.
The maximum coefficient that can occur in
\beta_{i}
is when we combine the coefficient
c_{p}
of some
\omega^{\gamma_{p}}
with
p\neq i
along with the coefficients of
\gamma_{i}\cdot(n-1)
and
D_{n}(\omega^{\gamma_{i}})
. This value can be at most
\ell+\ell(n-1)+\ell n(k-1)=\ell nk
, which proves the claim for this case.

Case 4:
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\omega^{\beta_{i}}\cdot c_{i}

In this case, if
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)
, we have that
\alpha^{\prime}=\oplus_{p\neq i}\omega^{\beta_{p}}\cdot c_{p}\oplus\omega^{\beta_{i}}\cdot(c_{i}-1)\oplus D_{n}(\omega^{\beta_{i}})
. By induction hypothesis, we have that

D_{n}(\omega^{\beta_{i}})
is
\ell nk
-lean. It then follows that the maximum coefficient
that can occur in
\alpha^{\prime}
is when we combine the coefficient
c_{p}
of some
\omega^{\gamma_{p}}
with
p\neq i
along with the coefficients of
D_{n}(\omega^{\beta_{i}})
.
This value can be at most
\ell+\ell nk
-lean, which proves the claim for this case.
∎

Proof of Lemma
31

Before, we give the proof for this lemma, we state some notation and recall some facts regarding ordinals.

For any ordinal
\alpha>0
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set
P_{n}(\alpha)=\beta
if
\alpha=\beta+1
and otherwise set
P_{n}(\alpha)=P_{n}(\alpha_{n})
where
\alpha_{n}
is the
n^{th}
element in the fundamental sequence of
\alpha
. From Proposition
30
, we get the following result.

Proposition 48
.

Let
\alpha<\Omega_{k+1}
be
\ell
-lean and
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)
.
Then for all
x\geq\ell+\ell nk
,
h_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\leq h_{P_{\ell+\ell nk}(\alpha)}(x)
.

Proof.

It is known that if
\beta<\alpha
and
\beta
is
m
-lean then for all
x\geq m
, we have
h_{\beta}(x)\leq h_{P_{m}(\alpha)}(x)

[
43
, Lemma B.1, Lemma C.9]
. Plugging in
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}
and
m=\ell+\ell nk
and using Proposition
30
, we get the required result.
∎

See
31

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\alpha
. If
\alpha=0
, then
M_{0}(n)=0=h_{0}(\ell nk)
.
Similarly, if
0<\alpha<\omega
, we can show that
M_{\alpha}(n)=\alpha=h_{\alpha}(\ell nk)
. Therefore, we can assume that
\alpha\geq\omega
and so
k\geq 1
.

By definition of
M_{\alpha,g}
, there must be some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\delta_{n}(\alpha)
such that

M_{\alpha,g}=1+M_{\alpha^{\prime},g}(g(n))

Since
\alpha
is
k
-lean, by Proposition
30
,
\alpha^{\prime}
is
\ell+\ell nk
-lean. We now apply the induction
hypothesis to
\alpha^{\prime}
to get

1+M_{\alpha^{\prime},g}(g(n))\leq 1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}((\ell+\ell nk)+(\ell+\ell nk)\cdot g(n)\cdot k)=1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}((\ell+\ell nk)\cdot(1+g(n)\cdot k))

Since
g
is a superadditive function, it follows that
1+g(n)\cdot k=1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}g(n)\leq 1+g(nk)
. Furthermore, since
g
is strictly increasing, it follows that
1+g(nk)\leq g(\ell+\ell nk)
.
Combining this observation with the fact that
h_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is monotonic, gives us that

1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}((\ell+\ell nk)\cdot(1+g(n)\cdot k))\leq 1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}((\ell+\ell nk)\cdot g(\ell+\ell nk))=1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}(h(\ell+\ell nk))

Observing that
h(\ell+\ell nk)\geq\ell+\ell nk
, if we now apply Proposition
48
, we get

1+h_{\alpha^{\prime}}(h(\ell+\ell nk))\leq 1+h_{P_{\ell+\ell nk}(\alpha)}(h(\ell+\ell nk))=h_{\alpha}(\ell+\ell nk)

where the last equality follows from the identity
1+h_{P_{x}(\alpha)}(h(x))=h_{\alpha}(x)

[
44
, Lemma 5.1]
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Appendix G
Proofs for Subsection
5.1

We now give the desired reflection from labelled
k
-depth graphs with labels from some set
Q

to labelled trees of height at most
k
with labels from
Q\times 2^{\{0,\dots,k-1\}}
.

The reflection
r
is as follows: Suppose
G
is a
k
-depth graph. This means that
G
is obtained by taking some tree
T
of height
k
and then adding some edges
(u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{\ell},v_{\ell})
, where each
v_{i}
is a descendant of
u_{i}
in
T
.
Let us call these the extra edges of
G
.

Hence, for each node
v
, we can assign the set
A(v)
which contains a number
i

iff the ancestor of
v
in the tree
T
at level
i
is connected to
v
by an extra edge.
Note that
A(v)\subseteq\{0,\dots,k-1\}
. Now, we set
r(G)=T^{\prime}

where
T^{\prime}
is the same as
T
, except the label of each node
v
in
T^{\prime}
is now a pair containing the label of
T
and
A(v)
. From the construction, it is clear that
|r(G)|=|G|
. Hence, we only need to show that
r(G)\leq_{is}r(G^{\prime})
implies
G\leq_{is}G^{\prime}
. To this end, suppose
T:=r(G)\leq_{is}T^{\prime}:=r(G^{\prime})
. This means that there is an injection from
T
to
T^{\prime}

such that
(u,v)\in T
iff
(h(u),h(v))\in T^{\prime}
. In other words, this means that there exists a subset
S
of nodes of
T^{\prime}
such that when
T^{\prime}
is restricted to
S
, we get
T
.
Similarly, let us now restrict
G^{\prime}
to the subset
S
to get a graph
H
. We claim that
H=G
,
which would prove that
G\leq_{is}G^{\prime}
and conclude the proof.

Indeed, since
T
and
G
have the same set of nodes, it follows that the nodes of
G
and the nodes of
H
are the same. We now have to show that the label of a node in
H
and
G
are the same and also that two nodes are connected by an edge in
H
iff they are connected by an edge in
G
.

First, let us pick some node
v\in S
. Suppose its label in
T^{\prime}
is
(q,S)
.
By construction, its label in
G^{\prime}
(and hence also in
H
) must be
q
.
Furthermore, by assumption, the label of
v
in
T^{\prime}
is the same as the label of
v
in
T
.
Hence, the label of
v
in
T
is
(q,S)
which implies that the label of
v
in
G
is also
q
.

Now, suppose we have an edge
(u,v)\in H
. By construction, this edge is also present in
G^{\prime}
. If it is also present in
T^{\prime}
, it must also be present in
T
and hence also in
G
.
Therefore, assume that this edge is not present in
T^{\prime}
. This implies that
(u,v)

is an extra edge of
G^{\prime}
. Let
u
be the ancestor of
v
at some level
i
in
T^{\prime}
. Since we have the extra edge
(u,v)
in
G^{\prime}
, the label of
v
in
T^{\prime}
must be
(q,S)
with
i\in S
. Therefore, the label of
v
in
T
must also be
(q,S)
with
i\in S
.
Furthermore, by assumption
u
is the ancestor of
v
at level
i
in
T
.
This indicates that
G
also has the extra edge
(u,v)
. Similarly, we can argue that if we have
an edge
(u,v)\in G
then
(u,v)\in H
as well. This proves that
H=G
and so we are done.

Appendix H
Proofs for Subsection
5.2

See
38

Note that the upper bound for these problems have already been discussed in Subsection
5.2
and so we will concentrate on the lower bounds here.
First, we formalize the intuition behind broadcast networks discussed in Subsection
5.2
.

H.1
k
-depth Broadcast Networks

Formally, a (broadcast) protocol is a tuple
\mathcal{P}=(Q,\Sigma,\delta,Q_{init})
where
Q
is a finite set of states,
\Sigma
is a finite communication alphabet,
\delta\subseteq Q\times\{!a,?a:a\in\Sigma\}\times Q
is a finite set of transitions and
Q_{init}\subseteq Q
is the set of initial states. For ease of notation, we will write
q\xrightarrow{!a}q^{\prime}
(resp.
q\xrightarrow{?a}q^{\prime}
) for
(q,!a,q^{\prime})\in\delta
(resp.
(q,?a,q^{\prime})\in\delta
). Intuitively, a transition of the form
q\xrightarrow{!a}q^{\prime}
(resp.
q\xrightarrow{?a}q^{\prime}
) corresponds to broadcasting the message
a
(resp. receiving the message
a
).

As discussed in Subsection
5.2
, a
k
-depth broadcast network consists of agents situated on the nodes of a
k
-depth graph and executing the protocol
\mathcal{P}
.
We formalize this as follows: A
k
-depth configuration
(or simply a configuration)
C=(V,E,L)

is a tuple such that
(V,E)
is a
k
-depth graph and
L:V\to Q

is a labelling function that specifies the current state of the agent in a node.
An initial configuration is a configuration in which all the nodes
have labels from
Q_{init}
.

The semantics of a
k
-depth broadcast network is given by means of steps between its configurations. Given two configurations
C=(V,E,L)
and
C^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
,
we say that there is a step between
C
and
C^{\prime}
if
V=V^{\prime},E=E^{\prime}

and there exists a node
v\in V
and a message
a\in\Sigma
such that

•

(L(v),!a,L^{\prime}(v))\in\delta
and

•

(L(v^{\prime}),?a,L^{\prime}(v^{\prime}))\in\delta
if
v^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
v
and

•

L(v^{\prime})=L^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
if
v^{\prime}
is not a neighbor of
v
.

In this case, we write
C\xrightarrow{v,a}C^{\prime}
or simply
C\xrightarrow{}C^{\prime}
. Intuitively, in such a step,
the agent at node
v
broadcasts the message
a
and this is simultaneously received by all of its neighbors; all the other agents do nothing.

A run from a configuration
C
to a configuration
C^{\prime}
is a sequence of steps

C\xrightarrow{}C_{1}\xrightarrow{}C_{2}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{}C^{\prime}
. We use
C\xrightarrow{*}C^{\prime}
to denote
that there exists a run between
C
and
C^{\prime}
. An execution is a run starting from some initial configuration.

Given a state
f\in Q
and a configuration
C
, we say that
C
covers
f
if

f
is the label of some node in
C
. We say that an execution
C_{0}\xrightarrow{*}C
covers

f
, if
C
covers
f
. The
coverability
problem for
k
-depth broadcast networks,
is to decide, given a broadcast protocol
\mathcal{P}
and a state
f
, whether
f
is coverable by some execution. The coverability problem for
k
-depth broadcast networks over trees is the same, except the configurations are now restricted to be
k
-depth trees, i.e.,
trees of height at most
k
.

We now show
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-hardness of the coverability problem for both these models
by giving a reduction from coverability for
k
-NRCS. To this end, we first need three ingredients regarding coverability in
k
-NRCS as well as coverability in
k
-depth broadcast networks, which we shall now discuss.

H.2
First Ingredient: Simple coverability for
k
-NRCS

The first ingredient that we shall need is the notion of simple coverability.
A simple coverability problem is just like the coverability problem, except that
the initial and final configurations are trees having one node labelled by states
q_{init}

and
q_{f}
respectively. If the coverability instance is true in this case, we simply say that
q_{init}
can cover
q_{f}
.
We can immediately see that the simple coverability problem
is as hard as the coverability problem, by standard reductions.

Indeed, let
\mathcal{N}
be any
k
-NRCS and let
C
be any configuration of
\mathcal{N}
.
Let
q_{r}
be the label of the root of
C
. We can now construct two gadgets

\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
and
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
with the following properties:

•

Both
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
and
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
contain all the states of
\mathcal{N}
(along with some more auxiliary states).

•

In
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
, starting from a configuration with a single node labelled by a state
q_{init}\notin\mathcal{N}
, we can reach a configuration
C^{\prime}
where the root is labelled by some state of
\mathcal{N}
iff
C^{\prime}=C
.

•

In
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
, starting from any configuration
C^{\prime}
, we can reach a configuration with the root labelled by a state
q_{f}\notin\mathcal{N}
iff
C^{\prime}
covers
C
.

Equipped with these two gadgets, it is now clear how to reduce coverability to simple coverability: Given an instance
(\mathcal{N},C,C^{\prime})
of coverablity, we construct
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C},\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C^{\prime}}
and then take our new
k
-NRCS to be
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
which first executes

\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
, then upon reaching a configuration where the root is labelled by some state in
\mathcal{N}
switches to executing
\mathcal{N}
and then
finally decides to non-deterministically switch to executing
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C^{\prime}}
at some point.
We then take our initial and final configurations to be trees with one node
labelled by
q_{init}
and
q_{f}
respectively. By the above properties, it is clear
that in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
the initial configuration can cover the final configuration
iff in
\mathcal{N}

C
can cover
C^{\prime}
.

All that is needed is to construct the above gadgets
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
and
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
.
To this end, let
B_{1},B_{2},\cdots,B_{n}
be the branches of
C
. Note that all of these branches share the root, whose state we shall take to be
q
.
Let
B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,B_{n}^{\prime}
be the same as these branches, except the label of the root
is now
(q^{\prime},1),\dots,(q^{\prime},n)
. In
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
, we will have the following transitions,
which successively add the branches
B_{1},B_{2},\cdots,B_{n}

\displaystyle(q_{init})\xrightarrow{u}B^{\prime}_{1}\qquad(q^{\prime},i)\xrightarrow{u}B^{\prime}_{i+1}\text{ for every }i\qquad(q^{\prime},n)\xrightarrow{u}q^{\prime}

Similarly, we can construct
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
to have the following transitions,
which successively remove the branches
B_{1},B_{2},\cdots,B_{n}

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u}(q^{\prime},n)\qquad B^{\prime}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{u}(q^{\prime},i)\text{ for every }i\qquad B^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{u}q_{f}

From the construction, it is clear that
\mathcal{N}_{fwd}^{C}
and
\mathcal{N}_{bwd}^{C}
satisfy their required properties.

H.3
Second Ingredient: Lossy semantics of
k
-NRCS

The next ingredient that we shall require is a new semantics for
k
-NRCS
called the
lossy semantics
. This is similar to the lossy semantics for
k
-NCS
[
12
,
4
]
and is explained below.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(Q,\delta_{u},\delta_{r})
. A
lossy step
of
\mathcal{N}
between two configurations

C
and
D
is said to occur if there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
C\geq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{}D
.
Intuitively, this means that we are first allowed to drop some subtrees from
C
to reach a configuration
C^{\prime}
and then take an usual step from there to
D
.
Using this notion of a lossy step relation, we can now define runs, reachability and coverability
in the same manner as was done using the usual
\xrightarrow{}
step relation.

Because of the compatibility property of
k
-NRCS (Proposition
20
), it immediately follows that a configuration
C
can cover a configuration
C^{\prime}
in the usual semantics of
\mathcal{N}
iff
C
can cover
C^{\prime}
in the lossy semantics of
\mathcal{N}
. This observation will be useful for our reduction.

H.4
Third Ingredient: Known reduction between
k
-NCS and
k
-depth broadcast networks

The final ingredient that we shall need is a known reduction between coverability in
k
-
NCS
, i.e.,
k
-NRCS without any reset transitions and coverability in
k
-depth broadcast networks. To this end, let
\mathcal{N}=(Q,\delta_{u})
be a
k
-NCS, (note that it has no resets). In
[
4
]
, it has been shown, how to start from
\mathcal{N}

and construct a
k
-depth broadcast protocol
\mathcal{P}
that satisfies certain properties.
We will now describe these properties, giving exactly enough detail that will be sufficient for our purposes.

States and configurations

\mathcal{P}
will have states and configurations
that satisfy the following properties:

•

For every state
q
of
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{P}
has states
of the form
(q,i)
for every
0\leq i\leq k
. Furthermore, it also has
states
(start,i)
and
(finish,i)
for every
0\leq i\leq k
. Such states
are called the good states of
\mathcal{P}
. Note that each one of these two states
is a pair, where the first element (called the base) belongs to
Q\cup\{start,finish\}
and the second element (called the grade) belongs to
\{0,1,\cdots,k\}
.
(
\mathcal{P}
will also have other auxiliary states, but they are not relevant for our discussion here).

•

A good configuration of
\mathcal{P}
is any configuration
C
such that 1)
C
is a tree of height at most
k
, 2)
C
is labelled only by good states, 3) the label of the root of
C
has grade 0 and, 4) every node
at distance
i
from the root has a label of grade
i
.

•

Every good configuration
C
of
\mathcal{P}
can be uniquely mapped
to a configuration
\mathbb{E}(C)
of
\mathcal{N}
as follows: First,
remove all the grades in all the labels of
C
and just keep the bases.
Next, remove all the nodes from
C
containing
start
or
finish
as their bases
and from the resulting forest, pick the tree containing the root.
In this way, we get a unique configuration
\mathbb{E}(C)
of
\mathcal{N}
.

Having described the states and configurations of
\mathcal{P}
that are of interest to us,
we now move on to describing the transitions of
\mathcal{P}
, which are defined relative
to transitions of
\mathcal{N}
.

Transitions

Let
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
be a transition of
\mathcal{N}
with
w=\max(i,j)
. For the sake of uniformity, if
i<j
, we let
p_{\ell}=start
for every
i<\ell\leq j
. Similarly, if
i>j
, we let
q_{\ell}=finish
for every
j<\ell\leq i
.
For every transition
t
of
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{P}
has a subset of transitions
S_{t}
over an alphabet

\{begin^{t}_{\ell},end^{t}_{\ell}:0\leq\ell\leq w\}
. Any transition in any subset
S_{t}
will always ensure the following properties.

•

Any transition of
S_{t}
will never change the grade of a node, i.e., if an agent was labelled
with some grade
g
before firing a transition, then its grade will remain
g
after firing.

•

No agent at a state with base
finish
can ever broadcast a message.

For the transition
t
, we say that a run between two good configurations
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
is
S_{t}
-good iff there exists a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
from the root in
\gamma
labelled by
(p_{0},0),\cdots,(p_{w},w)
such that the run between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is of the form

\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},begin^{t}_{0}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},begin^{t}_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{w}\xrightarrow{v_{w},begin^{t}_{w}}\beta

and

\beta\xrightarrow{v_{w},end^{t}_{w}}\beta_{w}\xrightarrow{v_{w-1},end^{t}_{w-1}}\beta_{w-1}\cdots\beta_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{0},end^{t}_{0}}\gamma^{\prime}

Having described the transitions of
\mathcal{P}
, we now move on to stating the following two

simulation properties
, which were proven in
[
4
]
.

•

Suppose
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
. Let
\gamma
be any good configuration of
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)=C
and
\gamma
has a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
from the root in
\gamma
labelled by
(p_{0},0),\cdots,(p_{w},w)
. Then, there is a
S_{t}
-good run from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
and the only difference between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}

is that the nodes
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
are now labelled by
(q_{0},0),\cdots,(q_{w},w)
.

•

Suppose
\gamma
is a good configuration and
\gamma\xrightarrow{*}\gamma^{\prime}
is a run such that

\gamma^{\prime}
is the first configuration after
\gamma
along the run in which the root is labelled by
a state with base
q\in Q
. Then,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a good configuration and the run between
\gamma

and
\gamma^{\prime}
is a
S_{t}
-good run for some transition
t
. Further, we also have
that there is a lossy step between
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)
and
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{N}
.

H.5
Our reduction from
k
-NRCS to
k
-depth broadcast networks

Using all the three ingredients from above, we now present our reduction from coverability in
k
-NRCS to coverability in
k
-depth broadcast networks.
By the first two ingredients it suffices to reduce from the simple coverability problem for

k
-NRCS over the lossy semantics.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(Q,\delta_{u},\delta_{r})
be a
k
-NRCS. We convert
\mathcal{N}
into a
k
-NCS
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
by taking every reset transition
t:=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})

and changing it into
t_{rename}:=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
.
We now use the reduction mentioned in the third ingredient to get
a protocol
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
for
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
that satisfies the simulation properties.

Recall that, for every transition
t^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
has a subset of transitions
S_{t^{\prime}}
over the alphabet
\{begin^{t}_{\ell},end^{t}_{\ell}:0\leq\ell\leq w\}
. We now modify
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}

as follows: For every transition of the form
t_{rename}:=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
(obtained form the reset transition
t:=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
), we consider the subset of transitions
S_{t_{rename}}
and we modify it by adding the following reception transition to it
(p,i+1)\xrightarrow{?end^{t_{rename}}_{i}}(finish,i+1)
.
We call this modified subset as
S_{t}
and call this new protocol
\mathcal{P}
.

Similar to the previous subsection, for any transition
t
of
\mathcal{N}
, we can define
the notion of an
S_{t}
-good run between good configurations
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
.
More precisely, let
t
be any transition of
\mathcal{N}
.
If
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
is an update transition let
w=\max(i,j)
.
If
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
is a reset transition let
w=i
.
Recall that we use
p_{i+1},\dots,p_{w}
to denote
start
and
q_{j+1},\dots,q_{w}
to denote
finish
.
With this in mind, we say that a run between two good configurations
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
is
S_{t}
-good iff there exists a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
from the root in
\gamma
labelled by
(p_{0},0),\cdots,(p_{w},w)
such that the run between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is of the form

\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},begin^{t}_{0}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},begin^{t}_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{w}\xrightarrow{v_{w},begin^{t}_{w}}\beta

and

\beta\xrightarrow{v_{w},end^{t}_{w}}\beta_{w}\xrightarrow{v_{w-1},end^{t}_{w-1}}\beta_{w-1}\cdots\beta_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{0},end^{t}_{0}}\gamma^{\prime}

We now prove various properties of
\mathcal{P}
.

H.5.1
Completeness properties of
\mathcal{P}

Using the first simulation property of
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
we can prove the following two
completeness
properties for
\mathcal{P}
.

•

Suppose
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
for some update transition
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{j})
with
w=\max(i,j)
.
Let
\gamma
be any good configuration of
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)=C
and
\gamma
has a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
from the root in
\gamma
labelled by
(p_{0},0),\cdots,(p_{w},w)
. Then, there is an
S_{t}
-good run from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
and the only difference between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is that the nodes
v_{0},\dots,v_{w}
are now labelled by
(q_{0},0),\cdots,(q_{w},w)
.

•

Suppose
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
for some reset transition
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
. Let
\gamma
be any good configuration of
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)=C
and
\gamma
has a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
from the root in
\gamma
labelled by
(p_{0},0),\cdots,(p_{i},i)
. Then, there is an
S_{t}
-good run from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
and the only difference between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}

is that the nodes
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
are now labelled by
(q_{0},0),\cdots,(q_{i},i)
and all the children
of
v_{i}
that were labelled by
(p,i+1)
are now labelled by
(finish,i+1)
.

The first one is immediate: If
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
for some update transition
t
,
then
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is also a step in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
. By the first simulation property of
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
,
we get the desired
S_{t}
-good run between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since the subset
S_{t}
is unchanged between
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{P}
, it follows that the same run is also an
S_{t}
-good run in
\mathcal{P}
.

The second one is proven as follows: Suppose
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
for some reset transition
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
. Let
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
be the path in
C
where
t
was applied.
Notice that
C\xrightarrow{t_{rename}}C^{\prime\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
where
C^{\prime\prime}
is the same as
C
, except there could be children of
v_{i}
labelled by
p
in
C^{\prime\prime}
. By the first simulation property of
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
, we get an
S_{t_{rename}}
-good run between
\gamma
and some configuration
\gamma^{\prime}

as follows:

\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},begin^{t}_{0}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},begin^{t}_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{i},begin^{t}_{i}}\beta

and

\beta\xrightarrow{v_{i},end^{t}_{i}}\beta_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{i-1},end^{t}_{i-1}}\beta_{i-1}\cdots\beta_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{0},end^{t}_{0}}\beta_{0}:=\gamma^{\prime}

If we fire the exact same steps from
\gamma
in
\mathcal{P}
, then we get the following
S_{t}
-good run

\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},begin^{t}_{0}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},begin^{t}_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{i}\xrightarrow{v_{i},begin^{t}_{i}}\beta

and

\beta\xrightarrow{v_{i},end^{t}_{i}}\beta_{i}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v_{i-1},end^{t}_{i-1}}\beta_{i-1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v_{0},end^{t}_{0}}\beta_{0}^{\prime}:=\gamma^{\prime\prime}

where each
\beta_{\ell}^{\prime}
is the same as
\beta_{\ell}
except that all the children of
v_{i}
that were labelled by
(p,i+1)
in
\beta_{\ell}
are now labelled by
(finish,i+1)
. It follows then that
we get a
S_{t}
-good run between
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
where
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
, which completes the proof of the second case.

H.5.2
Soundness properties of
\mathcal{P}

By a similar argument as the previous part, using the second simulation property of
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
, we can prove the following
soundness
property for
\mathcal{P}
.

Suppose
\gamma
is a good configuration and
\gamma\xrightarrow{*}\gamma^{\prime}
is a run such that

\gamma^{\prime}
is the first configuration after
\gamma
along the run in which the root is labelled by
a state with base
q\in Q
. Then,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a good configuration and the run between
\gamma

and
\gamma^{\prime}
is a
S_{t}
-good run for some transition
t
of
\mathcal{N}
. Furthermore,
there is a lossy step between
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)
and
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{N}
using
t
.

Indeed, let
\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},a_{0}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{v_{1},a_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{v_{n},a_{n}}\gamma_{n}:=\gamma^{\prime}
be such a run in
\mathcal{P}
. Compared to
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
, in
\mathcal{P}
we have only added
reception transitions, which could lead an agent to a state with base
finish
, from which no broadcasts
can ever be made. Hence, it must also be possible to get a run

\gamma\xrightarrow{v_{0},a_{0}}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v_{1},a_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{v_{n},a_{n}}\gamma_{n}^{\prime}:=\gamma^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
. By the second simulation property of
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
, this is a
S_{t^{\prime}}
-good run
for some transition
t^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
such that there is a lossy step between
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)
and
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
using
t^{\prime}
. We consider two cases:

•

Suppose
t^{\prime}=t
for some
t\in\mathcal{N}
. Hence, we have changed nothing in the sets

S_{t^{\prime}}
between
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{P}
. Therefore, each
\gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}=\gamma_{\ell}

and so the original run was also an
S_{t}
-good run of
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, this also implies that there is a lossy step between
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)
and
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{N}
using
t
.

•

Suppose
t^{\prime}=t_{rename}
for some reset transition

t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})\in\mathcal{N}

Notice then that the only difference between
\gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}
and
\gamma_{\ell}
might be that some states that were labelled by
(p,i+1)
in
\gamma^{\prime}_{\ell}
are now labelled by
(finish,i+1)
. Hence, this implies that the original run was an
S_{t}
-good run of
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, this also implies that there is a lossy step between
\mathbb{E}(\gamma)
and
\mathbb{E}(\gamma^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{N}
using
t
.

This completes the proof of the soundness property.

H.5.3
Wrapping up

Using these two properties, the following immediately follows
[
4
, Theorem 12]
.

Proposition 49
.

A state
q_{init}
can cover a state
q_{f}
in
\mathcal{N}
(in the sense of simple coverability) iff the state
(q_{f},0)

can be covered in the broadcast network
\mathcal{P}
from some good initial configuration, i.e., a good configuration where the root is labelled by
(q_{init},0)
and all children at level
i
are labelled by
(start,i)
.

Proof.

The right-to-left implication follows by taking a run in
\mathcal{P}
covering
(q_{f},0)
, splitting this run into parts where the root has a label with base
q\in Q

and then applying the completeness properties of
\mathcal{P}
to each of those parts.
(Recall that lossy coverability is the same as coverability).

The left-to-right implication is true by the following argument: Take a run

C_{0}\xrightarrow{t_{1}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{t_{N}}C_{N}

covering
q_{f}
from
q_{init}
in
\mathcal{N}
.
Take a good initial configuration
\gamma_{0}
such that each node has
N
children. By an induction on
i
from 0 to
N
and using the soundness property of
\mathcal{P}
, we can construct
a sequence of configurations
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N}
satisfying the following properties:

•

\mathbb{E}(\gamma_{i})=C_{i}

•

For each maximal branch
B=(p_{0},\dots,p_{j})
of
C_{i}
, there
is a unique branch
((p_{0},0),\dots,(p_{j},j))
of
\gamma_{i}
and from
the node labelled with
(p_{j},j)
, there are at least
(N-\ell)
different paths
labelled by
(start,j+1),\dots,(start,k)
.

•

Each
\gamma_{i}\xrightarrow{*}\gamma_{i+1}
.

This concludes the proof of the left-to-right implication and therefore of the proposition as well.
∎

Hence, we have given the desired reduction from simple coverability of
k
-NRCS over the lossy semantics to coverability of
k
-depth broadcast networks. This completes the proof of the desird lower bound for
k
-depth broadcast networks.

Appendix I
Proofs for Subsection
5.3

See
39

In order to prove this theorem, we first set up the formal syntax and semantics
of freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes.

I.1
Syntax and semantics of freeze LTL

Fix some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a finite set of atomic propositions
\Sigma

and an infinite set
\mathbb{D}
. A
k
-attributed data letter (or simply data letter) is a tuple
(A,d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
where
A\subseteq\Sigma
and each
d_{i}\in\mathbb{D}
. A data word is a sequence of data letters.

The syntax of freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes is all formulas generated according to the following grammar

\varphi::=a\ |\ \lnot\varphi\ |\ \varphi\land\varphi\ |\ X\ \varphi\ |\ \varphi\ U\ \varphi\ |\ G\varphi\ |\ F\varphi\ |\ \downarrow^{i}\varphi\ |\ \uparrow^{i}

where
a\in\Sigma
and
1\leq i\leq k
. The semantics of such formulas is defined
in terms of data words as follows.

We say that
\mathbf{d}
is a
partial data valuation
if
\mathbf{d}
is a function from
\{1,\dots,k\}
to
\mathbb{D}\cup\{\bot\}

where
\bot
is a special element not in
\mathbb{D}
. Intuitively a partial data valuation is like a
k
-tuple of values from
\mathbb{D}
, except that some of the values
can now be undefined (
\bot
). A complete data valuation
is a partial data valuation which does not use
\bot
in its range. Given any complete
data valuation
\mathbf{d}
and any
i\leq k
, we can get the partial data valuation

\mathbf{d}_{|i}
as the mapping such that
\mathbf{d}_{|i}(j)=\mathbf{d}(j)
if
j\leq i

and
\bot
otherwise.

Now, let
w=(A_{1},\mathbf{d_{1}}),(A_{2},\mathbf{d_{2}}),\dots,(A_{n},\mathbf{d_{n}})
be a data word where each
\mathbf{d_{j}}=(d_{j}^{1},\dots,d_{j}^{k})
. Let
1\leq i\leq n
and
\mathbf{d}
be a partial data valuation. We define the semantics of
(w,i,\mathbf{d})
satisfying a formula
\varphi
as follows

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models a

\displaystyle\iff\qquad a\in A_{i}

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models\lnot\varphi

\displaystyle\iff\qquad(w,i,\mathbf{d})\not\models\varphi

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\iff\qquad(w,i,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi_{1}\text{ and }(w,i,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models X\ \varphi

\displaystyle\iff\qquad(w,i+1,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models\varphi_{1}\ U\ \varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\exists j\in[i,n]\text{ s.t. }(w,j,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi_{2}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ell\in[i,j-1],(w,\ell,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models G\ \varphi

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\forall\ell\in[i,n],(w,\ell,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models F\ \varphi

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\exists\ell\in[i,n],(w,\ell,\mathbf{d})\models\varphi

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models\downarrow^{x}\varphi

\displaystyle\iff\qquad(w,i,\mathbf{d}_{i|x})\models\varphi

\displaystyle(w,i,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\models\uparrow^{x}

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\mathbf{d}_{|x}=\mathbf{d}_{i|x}

Given a freeze LTL formula with
k
-ordered attibutes, we say that it is satisfiable if there is some data
word
w
such that
(w,0,\mathbf{\bot})
satisfies it. Here
\mathbf{\bot}
is the
partial data valuation that maps everything in
\{1,\dots,k\}
to
\bot
.
The satisfiability problem is to decide if a given freeze LTL formula is satisfiable.

I.2
Upper Bound for Satisfiability of Freeze LTL

We now show an
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
upper bound for the satisfiability problem. To this end, we use a result of
[
12
, Theorem 12]
which showed that satisfiability for any formula in freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes can be converted to coverability for a
(k+1)
-NCS, i.e., a
(k+1)
-NRCS without any reset transitions.
The particular
(k+1)
-NCS that they construct has the property that
any configuration
C
reachable from the initial configuration
C_{init}
has
at most 2 children of the root
[
11
, Sections C.2 and C.3]
. We now show that any such
(k+1)
-NCS can be simulated by a
k
-NRCS as follows. The intuition is that in the
k
-NRCS that we construct, by means of resets, the root will simply simulate the 2 children without explicitly keeping them. This will allow us to save a level, thereby giving us a
k
-NRCS. We now explain this in detail.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(Q,\delta_{u})
be a
(k+1)
-NCS satisfying the property that in
any configuration
C
reachable from a configuration
C_{init}
, there are at most 2 children of the root. We will now construct a
k
-NRCS
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}=(Q^{\prime},\delta_{u}^{\prime},\delta_{r}^{\prime})

as follows: Let
\bot,\checkmark_{1},\checkmark_{2}
be elements not in
Q
and let
Q_{\bot}=Q\cup\{\bot\},M=\{\checkmark_{1},\checkmark_{2}\}
.
We set
Q^{\prime}=(Q\times Q_{\bot}\times Q_{\bot})\cup(Q\times M)\cup Q
.
Before we define the transitions
\delta^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
, we set up some notation.

I.2.1
Good configurations

A configuration
C^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
is called good if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The label of the root is of the form
(q,a,b)\in Q\times Q_{\bot}\times Q_{\bot}
.
Intuitively, the
a,b
keep track of the role of the 2 children of the root in
\mathcal{N}
.

•

The labels of the children of the root are of the form
(q,m)\in Q\times M
. Intuitively, if
m=\checkmark_{i}
, then this node would have been a child
of the
i^{th}
original child of the root in
\mathcal{N}
.

•

The labels of all of the other nodes belong to
Q
.

•

Suppose the label of the root is
(q,a,b)

–

If
a=b=\bot
then the root has no children.

–

If
a\neq\bot,b=\bot
, then the label of each child of the root is of the form
(q^{\prime},\checkmark_{1})
for some
q^{\prime}\in Q
. Similarly if
a=\bot,b\neq\bot
,
then the label of each child of the root is of the form
(q^{\prime},\checkmark_{2})
.

For each good configuration
C^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
,
we can assign a configuration
C:=\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{N}
as follows.
Let
(q,a,b)
be the label of the root
r
.

•

If
a=b=\bot
, then
C=C^{\prime}
, except the label of
r
is now just
q
.

•

Suppose exactly one of
a,b
is
\bot
(Without loss of generality, we can assume
b
is
\bot
. The construction is the same for the other case, except
\checkmark_{1}
below has to be replaced by
\checkmark_{2}
). Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be the children of the root
r

with labels
(q_{1},\checkmark_{1}),\dots,(q_{n},\checkmark_{1})
.
Then we construct
C
as follows:
We first add a new child
v
to
r
and replace every edge
(r,v_{i})

with
(v,v_{i})
.
Then, we label the root as
q
, label the new child
v
as
a
,
label each node
v_{i}
as
q_{i}
and
keep the other nodes as it is.

•

Suppose
a,b\in Q
.
Let
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}
be the children of the root
r
with labels

(p_{1},\checkmark_{1}),\dots,(p_{m},\checkmark_{1})
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be the children of the root
r

with labels
(q_{1},\checkmark_{2}),\dots,(q_{n},\checkmark_{2})
. Then we construct
C
as follows:
We first add two new children
u,v
to
r
and replace edges
(r,u_{i})

with
(u,u_{i})
and edges
(r,v_{i})
with
(v,v_{i})
respectively.
Then, we label the root as
q
, label the new children
u,v
as
a,b
respectively,
label each node
u_{i}
