as
p_{i}
, label each node
v_{i}
as
q_{i}
and
keep the other nodes as it is.

I.2.2
Simulation properties

We will now specify the transitions of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
. Before that,
we first define a new type of transition called
generalized reset transition
.
A generalized reset transition is a transition of the form

t:=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})

where
S
is any subset of states. The semantics of such a transition
t
is given as follows:
We say that
C\xrightarrow{t}C^{\prime}
if there is a path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
in
C
starting at the root
such that for each
0\leq\ell\leq i
,
v_{\ell}
is labelled by
p_{\ell}
and
C^{\prime}
is obtained
from
C
by 1) for every
0\leq\ell\leq i
, changing the label of
v_{\ell}
to
q_{\ell}

and 2) removing every subtree rooted at a child
v
of
v_{i}
such that
v
has a label from
S
.
Intuitively, a generalized reset transition allows us to perform a reset on a subset
S
of labels simultaneously.

From the semantics, it is clear that any generalized reset transition can be simulated by a series of reset transitions; for instance if
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,S$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
with
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}
, then this can be replaced by

m
transitions
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}
with

t_{1}=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,s_{1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(t^{1},\dots,t^{1})

t_{j+1}=(t^{j},\dots,t^{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{j+1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{j+1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,s_{j+1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{j+1}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(t^{j+1},\dots,t^{j+1})

for every
1\leq j<m-1
and then finally

t_{m}=(t^{m-1},\dots,t^{m-1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{m}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{m}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,s_{m}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,s_{m}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})

For the sake of conciseness, in our definition of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
, we will use generalized reset transitions. For each transition
t
of
\mathcal{N}
, we will construct a subset of transitions
S_{t}
of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
such that the following simulation properties hold:

•

Suppose
C\xrightarrow{t}D
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
. Then
for any
C^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime})=C
, there exists
D^{\prime}

such that
\mathbb{E}(D^{\prime})=D
and
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}D^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}\in S_{t}

•

Suppose
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}D^{\prime}
is a step in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}

for some
t^{\prime}\in S_{t}
such that
C^{\prime}

is a good configuration. Then
D^{\prime}
is a good configuration and

\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime})\xrightarrow{t}\mathbb{E}(D^{\prime})
is a step in
\mathcal{N}
.

These two properties ensure that a configuration
C_{f}
can be covered in
\mathcal{N}
from
C_{init}
iff
some configuration
C^{\prime}_{f}
such that
\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime}_{f})=C_{f}
can be covered from some configuration
C^{\prime}_{init}
such that
\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime}_{init})=C_{init}
in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
.
For any configuration
C
, note that there are only a fixed number of configurations

C^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime})=C
. Hence, these two properties would then show that coverability in
\mathcal{N}
can be solved by a constant number of coverability queries in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
, which we know is in
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
, thereby giving the required bound
Hence, it only suffices to construct transitions
\delta^{\prime}
that satisfy the above simulation properties.

I.2.3
Transitions of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}

Let
t=(p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{j})
be a transition of the
(k+1)
-NRCS
\mathcal{N}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that if
j=0
then
i=1
. Indeed, if
j=0
and
i>1
then
t
can be replaced by
t^{\prime}=(p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},\dots,p_{i}^{\prime})
and
t^{\prime\prime}=(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(p_{0}^{\prime})
.

Recall the intuition that, in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
, the root will also simulate the role of its children (which in this case corresponds to
p_{1},q_{1}
).
Corresponding to this intuition,
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
will have the following transitions
S_{t}
, which we divide into multiple cases.

Case 1:
i=0

In this case, we would have the following transitions
S_{t}
:

((p_{0},a,\bot))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},a,q_{1}),(q_{2},\checkmark_{2}),q_{3},\dots,q_{j})

(6)

((p_{0},\bot,b))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},q_{1},b),(q_{2},\checkmark_{1}),q_{3},\dots,q_{j})

(7)

Further if
j=0
we would also have

((p_{0},a,b))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},a,b))

(8)

It can be easily seen that
S_{t}
satisfies the required simulation properties in this case.

Case 2:
i\geq 1,j\geq 1

In this case, we would have the following transitions
S_{t}
:

((p_{0},p_{1},b),(p_{2},\checkmark_{1}),p_{3},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},q_{1},b),(q_{2},\checkmark_{1}),q_{3},\dots,q_{j})

(9)

((p_{0},a,p_{1}),(p_{2},\checkmark_{2}),p_{3},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},b,q_{1}),(q_{2},\checkmark_{2}),q_{3},\dots,q_{j})

(10)

It can be easily seen that
S_{t}
satisfies the required simulation properties in this case.

Case 3:
i\geq 1,j=0

In this case, as remarked before, we can assume that
i=1
. In this case, we would have the following transitions

S_{t}
:

((p_{0},p_{1},b),(p_{2},\checkmark_{1}))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{1}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{1}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{1}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{1}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},\bot,b))

(11)

((p_{0},a,p_{1}),(p_{2},\checkmark_{2}))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{2}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{2}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{2}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,gr,(Q\times\{\checkmark_{2}\})$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}((q_{0},a,\bot))

(12)

It can be easily seen that
S_{t}
satisfies the required simulation properties in this case.

Hence, this completes the construction of the transitions of
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
satisfying the simulation properties. As discussed before, this is sufficient to reduce coverability in
\mathcal{N}
to a constant number of instances of coverability in
\mathcal{N^{\prime}}
.
This shows the required upper bound.

I.3
Lower Bound for Satisfiability of Freeze LTL

We now show an
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
lower bound for the satisfiability problem.
To this end, we use a result of
[
12
, Theorem 13]
which gives a reduction
from coverability in
k
-NCS to satisfiability of freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes.
This result uses an encoding of runs of a
k
-NCS by means of data words.
We recall that enocding here and show that it can be adapted to also handle
runs of
k
-NRCS.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(Q,\delta_{u},\delta_{r})
be a
k
-NRCS. As stated in Subsubsection
H.3
, coverability over the lossy semantics in
\mathcal{N}
is the same as coverability over the usual semantics in
\mathcal{N}
and so it suffices to only concentrate on lossy runs of
\mathcal{N}
.
Note that any lossy run of
\mathcal{N}
can be written down as a sequence of configurations
C_{0},C_{0}^{\prime},C_{1},C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{n},C_{n}^{\prime}
where each
C_{i}^{\prime}\leq_{is}C_{i}
and each
C_{i}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{i}}C_{i+1}
for some transition
t_{i}\in\delta_{u}\cup\delta_{r}
.
We now build an encoding of such a run as a data word in a step-by-step manner as follows.

I.3.1
Encoding a single configuration.

We say that a data word
w
is an encoding of a configuration
C
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

w
contains exactly
2b
many letters where
b
is the number of maximal branches of
C
.

•

Every odd letter of
w
contains the atomic proposition
odd

and every even letter of
w
contains the atomic proposition
even
.

•

For every maximal branch
B=(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
of
C
there are exactly two letters of
w
given by

odd_{B}=(X,d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
and
even_{B}=(Y,d_{1}^{\prime},\dots,d_{k}^{\prime})
, where
odd_{B}
appears in an odd position and
even_{B}
appears next to
odd_{B}
.

•

The sets
X
and
Y
in
odd_{B}
and
even_{B}
contain the atomic propositions
(q_{0},0),(q_{1},1),\dots,(q_{i},i),(-,i+1),\dots,(-,k)
. Furthermore, no other atomic
propositions of the form
(q,j)
are present for any
0\leq j\leq k
.

•

The data values of
odd_{B}
and
even_{B}
are mutually exclusive, i.e., no
d_{j}

is equal to any
d_{\ell}^{\prime}
.

•

For any two maximal branches
B,B^{\prime}
and any
1\leq j\leq k
,
the
j^{th}
data value
of
odd_{B}
and
odd_{B^{\prime}}
are the same iff they share a common prefix
of length
j
from the root. Similarly, for
even_{B}
and
even_{B^{\prime}}
.

Now, note that these conditions stay the same irrespective of whether
\mathcal{N}
contains reset transitions or not. Therefore, if we let
\mathcal{N}_{no-r}
be the same as
\mathcal{N}

except it has no reset transitions, then a data word encodes a configuration of
\mathcal{N}_{no-r}

iff it encodes the same configuration of
\mathcal{N}
. Since we know that
there is a formula of freeze LTL which is satisfied by exactly words encoding configurations
[
11
, Section D.1]
, it follows that

Proposition 50
.

There is a formula
\Phi_{Conf}
of freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes such that

\Phi_{Conf}
is satisfied by a word
w
iff it is an encoding of some configuration
of
\mathcal{N}
.

I.3.2
Encoding a lossy step

Using the above encoding, we will now encode a lossy step of the machine
\mathcal{N}
, i.e., we will encode a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})

which satisfies the conditions that there exists
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C
.
(Note that
C
is the configuration we move
to
after firing t).

For any transition
t\in\delta_{u}
(i.e., a non-reset transition of
\mathcal{N}
), we say that a word
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

w
contains at least
3
occurrences of a letter of the form
(\{\$\},d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
. Such letters are called special letters.

•

The subword of
w
in between the first and second occurrence
of a special letter encodes
C
. The subword of
w
in between
the second and third occurrence of a special letter encodes
C^{\prime}
.

•

There exists
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C
.
Furthermore, suppose
v_{0},\dots,v_{j}
is the path along which
t
was applied to in
C
.
Then

–

If a maximal branch
B
of
C
contains a prefix of this path up till depth
1\leq\ell\leq j
, then the letter
even_{B}
in the encoding of
C
in
w
also contains the atomic propositions
t,\checkmark_{1},\dots,\checkmark_{\ell}
.

–

No other letter contains any of the atomic propositions
t,\checkmark_{1},\dots,\checkmark_{k}
.

By
[
11
, Sections D.2 and D.3]
, we have that

Proposition 51
.

For any transition
t\in\delta_{u}
, there is a formula
\Phi_{t-step}
which is satisfied by a word
w
iff
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
.

Now, we show that such a formula can also be obtained for any reset transition
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
. Corresponding to the reset transition
t
, we define a non-reset transition
t_{rename}=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,u$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
. Note that
t_{rename}
need not be in
\mathcal{N}
.
However,
t_{rename}
satisfies the following useful property.

Proposition 52
.

For any
C,C^{\prime}
, there exists
C^{\prime\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C

iff there exists
D^{\prime\prime}
such that
D^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{rename}}C

and along the path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
in
C
where
t_{rename}
was applied to, there is no child of
v_{i}
labelled by
p
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists
C^{\prime\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C
.
Let
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
be the path along which
t
is fired in
C^{\prime\prime}
.
We construct
D^{\prime\prime}
by taking
C^{\prime\prime}
and removing all the sub-trees rooted at the children of
v_{i}
labelled by
p
. Then from
D^{\prime\prime}
we fire
t_{rename}
along the
path
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
. By construction we have
D^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{rename}}C

and along the path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
in
C
where
t_{rename}
was applied to, there is no child of
v_{i}
labelled by
p
.

Suppose there exists
D^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{rename}}C

and along the path
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
in
C
where
t_{rename}
was applied to, there is no child of
v_{i}
labelled by
p
. We simply take
C^{\prime\prime}=D^{\prime\prime}
and apply
t
along the
same path. In this way, it is clear that
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C
and so we are done.
∎

Motivated by this proposition, we make the following definition: For any reset transition
t
, we say that a word
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

w
contains exactly
3
occurrences of a letter of the form
(\{\$\},d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
. Such letters are called special letters.

•

The subword of
w
in between the first and second occurrence
of a special letter encodes
C
. The subword of
w
in between
the second and third occurrence of a special letter encodes
C^{\prime}
.

•

There exists
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{rename}}C
.
Furthermore, suppose
v_{0},\dots,v_{i}
is the path along which
t_{rename}
was applied to in
C
.
Then

–

If a maximal branch
B
of
C
contains a prefix of this path up till depth
1\leq\ell\leq i
, then the letter
even_{B}
in the encoding of
C
in
w
contains the atomic propositions
t_{rename},\checkmark_{1},\dots,\checkmark_{\ell}
.

–

If a maximal branch
B
of
C
contains a prefix of this path up till depth
i
, then the letter
even_{B}
in the encoding of
C
in
w
does not contain the atomic proposition
(p,i+1)
.

–

No other letter contains any of the atomic propositions
t_{rename},\checkmark_{1},\dots,\checkmark_{k}
.

From the above definition, it is clear that the following property called
Property A

holds:

Suppose
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
is a reset transition. A word
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
iff
w
encodes the tuple
(C,t_{rename},C^{\prime})
and for every letter
even_{B}
appearing in the encoding of
C
in
w
containing the
atomic propositions
t_{rename},\checkmark_{1},\dots,\checkmark_{i}
,

even_{B}
must not have
(p,i+1)
as a proposition.

Therefore, using Property A and Proposition
52
we get,

Proposition 53
.

For ant reset transition
t
, a word
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
iff there exists

C^{\prime\prime}
such that
C^{\prime\prime}\leq_{is}C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{t}C
.

Furthermore, because of Property A we can easily show that

Proposition 54
.

For any reset transition
t
, there is a formula
\Phi_{t-step}
which is satisfied by a word
w
iff
w
encodes a tuple
(C,t,C^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
t=(p_{0},\dots,p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,r,p$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}(q_{0},\dots,q_{i})
.
By Property A,
\Phi_{t-step}
can simply be defined as the conjunction of
\Phi_{t_{rename}-step}

and
G((even\land t\land\bigwedge_{1\leq\ell\leq i}\checkmark_{\ell})\implies\lnot(p,i+1))
.
∎

I.3.3
Encoding lossy runs

We can now use the encoding a lossy step to encode lossy runs. Indeed, we say that a word
w
encodes a sequence
C_{n},t_{n},C_{n-1},t_{n-1},C_{n-2},\dots,t_{1},C_{0}
if

•

w
has at least
n+2
special letters.

•

The word between any
(i+1)^{th}
special letter and
(i+3)^{th}
special
letter encodes the tuple
(C_{n-i},t_{n-i},C_{n-i-1})
.

Note that a word
w
can encode a sequence
C_{n},t_{n},C_{n-1},\dots,t_{0},C_{0}
iff
there is a lossy run of
\mathcal{N}
of the form
C_{n}\geq_{is}C_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{n}}C_{n-1}\geq_{is}C_{n-1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t_{n-1}}\dots\xrightarrow{t_{0}}C_{0}
.

It is easy to see that we can build a formula that recognizes only words
w
encoding such sequences. Indeed, we just need to specify that any subword of
w
starting at
\$
must encode a lossy step, except for the last two
\$
, which can be done using the formula

\Phi_{seq}:=G(\$\implies\bigvee_{r\in\delta}\Phi_{r-step}\lor\lnot XF\$\lor\lnot(X(F(\$\land\lnot XF\$)))

I.3.4
Reducing simple coverability of
k
-NRCS to satisfiability

We now reduce simple coverability of
k
-NRCS
to satisfiability of freeze LTL formulas. Recall that in the simple coverability problem,
we are given a
k
-NRCS
\mathcal{N}
and two states
q_{init}
and
q_{f}
and we want
to checke if starting from a configuration with a single node labelled by
q_{init}

we can reach a configuration covering a single node labelled by
q_{f}
. As shown in Subsection
H.2
, this is
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-hard.

Note that the answer to the simple coverability problem is yes iff there is a data word

w
encoding a sequence
C_{n},t_{n},C_{n-1},\cdots,t_{0},C_{0}
with
C_{n}
being a configuration where the root is labelled by
q_{f}
and
C_{0}
being a configuration with a single node labelled by
q_{init}
. Therefore, we just need to take the formula
\Phi_{seq}

from the previous part and attach to it the following two constraints:

•

The second letter of the word encoding the sequence must represent a branch of a configuration whose root node is labelled by
q_{f}
(Remember that the first letter of the word must be a special letter with
\$
)

X((q_{f},0))

•

The penultimate letter of the word encoding the sequence must represent the initial configuration (Remember that the last letter of the word must be a special letter with
\$
)

(X(\$)\land\lnot XF(\$))\implies((q_{init,0})\land\bigwedge_{q\in Q,1\leq\ell\leq k}\lnot(q,\ell))

By the property of the formula
\Phi_{seq}
, it follows that
\Phi_{seq}
along
with these constraints are satisfiable by a word iff the answer to the simple coverability problem is yes. This completes the required reduction
and proves the
\mathbf{F}_{\Omega_{k}}
-hardness of satisfiability of freeze LTL with
k
-ordered attributes.
